1

A BSTRACT
Language models are becoming infrastructure, and infrastructure built in
the open, by public institutions, on documented data, is the alternative
to dependence on a few providers; Apertus shows this is viable at scale.
Apertus 1.5 extends the fully open Apertus family from text to multimodal
reasoning. Continued pretraining of the Apertus 1.0 models, on 4T additional tokens for the 8B and 2T for the 70B, adds image and audio understanding and extends the context window fourfold to 262,144 tokens; multimodality enters through the tokenizer, the language-model head remains
restricted to text, and the architecture is unchanged. Supervised finetuning, long-context finetuning, reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, and preference alignment complete the released models. Thinking
is a flag of the chat template rather than a separate model, and lifts mathematical reasoning by up to 20 points on the 8B model. The released models
improve over their predecessors on nearly every benchmark; among fully
open models of its class, the 8B leads on knowledge, instruction following,
and cultural benchmarks, and its instruction following surpasses all compared models, including open-weight ones. On image and audio understanding, a first for the family, the models are competitive with fully open
multimodal models, strongest on document understanding and long-form
and lower-resource speech recognition, while specialized vision and audio
models remain ahead on perception and transcription of English and other
high-resource languages. As in Apertus 1.0, we train only on openly available data, respect crawler opt-outs retroactively, and exclude restrictively
licensed material. We release the model weights, data preparation and
training recipes, and evaluation suites under permissive licenses; openness extends to values, with the Apertus charter guiding alignment stages
published alongside the models.

1

I NTRODUCTION

The most capable large language models (LLMs) are accessible only through proprietary
interfaces, and even the strongest openly released models typically publish their weights
without the data, code, recipes, and values behind them. Fully open models are increasingly rare as the complexity and cost of LLM development have grown significantly, and
they still trail their open-weight peers in capabilities. Apertus exists to close this gap. We
understand full openness to extend across five axes: open weights, open data, open code,
open recipes, and open values. Every claim in this report can therefore be traced to released artifacts, and the principles that shape the behavior of our models are published
documents rather than internal policy.
Apertus is a collaborative effort led by ETH Zürich, EPFL, and CSCS, with contributors
from many further institutions, to develop AI in an inclusive and trustworthy manner,
and to provide a foundation on which developers build products and services. Pluralism is a founding goal: from the first release onward, Apertus pursues broad coverage of
languages and cultures, and this focus continues in the present release. A second commitment is responsible development, guided by Swiss and European ethical and regulatory
standards, while striving for performance and scale. LLMs are a central technology of
our time, and we believe research institutions should engage with them directly. Apertus
provides a scientific basis for research and development on open models by making its
model weights, code, data, training recipe, and values available for study and reuse. This
enables researchers and practitioners to examine the training process, deploy models independently, retain control over associated data flows, and develop the expertise needed to
teach the next generation of engineers and researchers.
We released the first generation of models, Apertus 1.0, with 8B and 70B parameters in
September 2025, trained from scratch on 15T tokens of openly documented text spanning
over 1000 languages on the Alps supercomputer at CSCS (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026).
Its data pipeline respected machine-readable opt-outs of domain owners, applying them
retroactively to earlier crawls, removed personally identifiable information, and drew only
from permissively licensed sources, and training suppressed verbatim memorization. The
Apertus charter, published alongside the models, states the principles that guide their behavior, and the release included transparency documentation following the EU AI Act template, intermediate checkpoints, data preparation pipelines, and training code.
Apertus 1.5 8B and Apertus 1.5 70B build on this foundation through continued pretraining on a mix of text, image, and audio data, roughly 4T tokens for the 8B model and 2T tokens for the 70B model. Where Apertus 1.0 established that fully open training at scale is
possible, Apertus 1.5 expands and improves the capabilities of the models. The models
accept image and audio inputs in addition to text, support an optional thinking mode for
harder reasoning problems, extend the context window fourfold to 262,144 tokens, follow
instructions more reliably, and use tools more effectively. Figure 1 gives an overview of the
complete training pipeline that produces both models, from the Apertus 1.0 checkpoints
through continued pretraining and post-training to the released models. Figure 2 gives
an overview of the shared model architecture and how it processes text, image, and audio
inputs.
Our main contributions are:
• Apertus 1.5 8B and Apertus 1.5 70B. Two fully open models that bring image and
audio understanding, an optional thinking mode, a fourfold longer context window, and stronger instruction following and tool use to the Apertus family.
• Multimodal continued pretraining. A recipe that upgrades the Apertus 1.0 family
in place: multimodality enters through the tokenizer, the language-model head
remains restricted to text tokens, and the architecture is preserved.
• Long-context extension. The context window grows fourfold to 262,144 tokens,
supported by dedicated long-context stages in both continued pretraining and
post-training.

Figure 1: The Apertus 1.5 training pipeline. Solid blue stages train on multimodal data
(T · I · A, or T · I for RLVR), light-blue stages on text only (T); red outlines mark the released
models, which the 70B reaches without SDPO. DPO uses online pairs for the 8B model and
offline and online pairs for the 70B model.
• Our next generation of post-training. Supervised finetuning (SFT), long-context
SFT, reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), direct preference optimization (DPO), and charter alignment with self-distillation policy optimization
(SDPO), delivering the optional thinking mode, more reliable instruction following, and more effective tool use.
• Full release. Weights, intermediate checkpoints, data preparation pipelines, training code, and evaluation harnesses, released under the Apache 2.0 license.
The remainder of this report follows the lifecycle of the models. Section 2 presents the multimodal tokenization, Section 3 the pretraining data and the continued-pretraining recipe,
and Section 4 the post-training pipeline. Section 5 reports evaluations across modalities
and languages, Section 6 describes the training infrastructure and its efficiency, Section 7
discusses the release and its governance, and Section 8 concludes.
1.1

R ELEASED M ODELS , C ODE , AND D ATASETS

Full openness means every stage of this report can be retraced from released artifacts. We
group the release into models, code, and datasets; all code and data carry permissive licenses, and the models are distributed under Apache 2.0.
Models. The two final post-trained models are distributed on Hugging Face behind a
gated download that records assent to the acceptable-use policy and reaches deployers
with updates (Section 7.1); major intermediate checkpoints live as branches of these two
repositories. The post-training stage checkpoints, one repository per stage under the same
license and gating, let the lineage of Section 5.3.2 be inspected checkpoint by checkpoint.
Thinking is a request-time flag of the chat template rather than a separate model, even
where inference providers expose it as a separate endpoint; usage instructions and supported inference stacks are in the model cards.

multimodal pretraining and SFT extensions, the multimodal tokenizer, the data ingestion
inventory, the post-training framework, and the text and multimodal evaluation harnesses.
Further special-purpose repositories, such as the tokenizer benchmarks and the synthetic
long-context task generator, are linked in the sections where they are used.

2

M ULTIMODALITY

One core objective of Apertus 1.5 is to extend the models’ capabilities to understanding
and working with images and audio. We train both Apertus 1.5 8B and Apertus 1.5 70B to
understand images and audio and respond in text. The multimodal effort spans continued
pretraining, supervised finetuning, and long-context supervised finetuning (Figure 1).
This chapter describes how the two new modalities enter the model. Section 2.1 sets out the
discrete-token representation and the reasons we prefer it to modality-specific encoders,
while Section 2.1.1 and Section 2.1.2 specify the two tokenizers with the vocabulary they
contribute. Section 2.2 presents the staged multimodal training data used throughout the
pipeline, whereas Section 2.3 describes the tokenization pipelines and their integration into
the training infrastructure together with the modifications of the pretraining and SFT codebase to enable multimodal training. Finally, Section 2.4 introduces ablations on preserving
text capabilities while adding modalities during continued pretraining, and Appendix B
and Appendix C record the data pool of each training stage. The following chapter, Section 3, presents the continued-pretraining recipe.
2.1

U NIFIED R EPRESENTATION

We represent images and audio as discrete tokens, following unified models such as
Chameleon and Emu3 (Chameleon Team, 2024; Wang et al., 2026). A shared vocabulary
represents all modalities, and the language model applies a single next-token-prediction
objective across them, as illustrated in Figure 2.
A common alternative is to use modality-specific encoders whose features are projected
into the language model, as in LLaVA (Liu et al., 2023). More recent unified approaches instead avoid separate pretrained modality-specific encoders and process continuous inputs
directly: SenseNova-U1 (Cai et al., 2026) operates directly on pixels and text in a unified
end-to-end architecture, while the encoder-free Gemma 4 12B (Gemma Team et al., 2026) directly projects raw image patches and raw audio into the model’s embedding space. These
approaches unify multimodal computation within a single model, but represent non-text
inputs continuously rather than through discrete modality tokens.
Our discrete representation allows the same next-token-prediction objective to be applied
to images and audio, enabling the first stage of continued pretraining to draw on unpaired
images and audio (Irmiger et al., 2026). In this stage, the model learns the new modalities
from image-only and audio-only examples alongside text, before learning their alignment
with language from paired data in the subsequent stage (Figure 1). Image and audio codes
serve directly as prediction targets, preserving a common training objective across modalities. A controlled study shows that an image-only stage before paired training can improve
image understanding on most benchmark groups (Kreft, 2026); Section 2.4 summarizes its
findings. Unpaired data also needs no captions, so it remains usable where captions are
scarce or of low quality.
We convert training images and audio into discrete tokens once, during offline preprocessing (Section 2.1.1, Section 2.1.2), just as we precompute text tokens. We reuse these
tokenized datasets across subsequent training runs and experiments that use the same
tokenizers and preprocessing. The language model consumes the precomputed tokens
through its shared embedding layer; no vision or audio encoder runs in its forward pass.
This separation lets us reuse the text-training infrastructure without the overhead and complexity of encoder forward passes during training, achieving throughput comparable to
text-only training with only minor modality-specific modifications to the pretraining and
SFT codebases (Section 2.3). A measured comparison substantiates the efficiency benefit: at
an identical configuration (256 nodes, 8,192-token sequences), text-only ablation runs sustain 5,300 to 5,500 tokens per second per GPU, and the first multimodal production stage
5,474 (Section 6.2).

reserved multimodal slots, including structure markers, followed by 131,072 image codes

2.1.1

I MAGES

We use the vision tokenizer of Emu3.5 (Cui et al., 2025b) to convert training images into discrete tokens during offline preprocessing. Irmiger et al. (2026) evaluate image tokenizers
on reconstruction quality, on the average number of tokens per image, and on their support for arbitrary image shapes, and find that the tokenizers of the Emu family combine
a favorable trade-off between reconstruction quality and token efficiency with native support for arbitrary shapes. Section 2.4 discusses the choice between the Emu3 and Emu3.5
tokenizers.1
The adopted tokenizer is trained solely with a pixel-reconstruction objective, rather than
an explicit semantic or image-text alignment objective as used by vision encoders such as
CLIP (Radford et al., 2021). We use its pretrained discrete codes directly as inputs and
prediction targets for the language model.
1 We provide code and benchmarks at swiss-ai/benchmark-image-tokenizer.

10

The tokenizer maps every block of 16 × 16 pixels to one code from a codebook of 131,072 entries of dimension 256. An image of 512 × 512 pixels thus becomes a grid of 32 × 32 codes,
or 1,024 tokens, and an image of arbitrary shape becomes a grid of its height and width
each divided by 16. The codebook is trained with index backpropagation quantization,
which keeps a codebook of this size in use where earlier vector quantization leaves most
codes dead (Shi et al., 2025).
In the token sequence, an image opens with a start marker followed by its dimensions;
its rows of codes are separated by end-of-row markers, and an end marker closes it. The
visual codes extend the vocabulary directly after the text tokens of Apertus 1.0 and a block
of 200 reserved slots, seven of which hold these structure markers.
2.1.2

A UDIO

We use the WavTokenizer codec (Ji et al., 2025) to convert training audio into discrete tokens
during offline preprocessing. The codec discretizes a waveform with a single quantizer
over a codebook of 4,096 entries. It consumes mono audio at 24 kHz and emits one code
per 600 samples, hence 40 tokens per second; a minute of audio costs 2,400 tokens. Inputs
at other sampling rates are resampled. Because the codec was trained on peak-normalized
audio, we normalize every input to a peak of −3 dBFS before tokenization.
Neural audio codecs such as EnCodec quantize each frame with a stack of residual codebooks (Défossez et al., 2023), and their parallel code streams force an autoregressive model
to interleave them (Copet et al., 2023). WavTokenizer emits one code per frame, so audio
enters the model as a single flat sequence, exactly like text. This single code stream is the
deciding criterion, as it leaves the training stack unchanged.2
In the token sequence, an audio segment is enclosed by start and end markers, which live
in the same reserved block of the vocabulary as their visual counterparts. The audio codes
follow the visual codes in the extended vocabulary.
2.2

M ULTIMODAL T RAINING D ATA

Multimodal training data is central to this release. It teaches the models to use the image
and audio token spaces introduced above. It also aligns both modalities with language and
supports multimodal instruction following across continued pretraining and post-training.
Across the three continued-pretraining stages, text-only data accounts for roughly 60% of
the Apertus 1.5 8B training mix and 55% of the Apertus 1.5 70B mix, with vision and audio
data accounting for the remaining 40% and 45%, respectively (Table 3). We organize the
discussion around the six data stages that serve these different roles, a design supported
by the ablation of Section 2.4. The six stages are the three continued-pretraining stages
of Section 3.2, whose third stage is the cooldown, followed by long-context pretraining
(Section 3.3), supervised finetuning (Section 4.2), and long-context supervised finetuning
(Section 4.3).
2.2.1

C URATION AND P ROCESSING

Acquisition and Normalization. The public repository swiss-ai/multimodal-data
provides an inventory of vision and audio datasets, recording each source’s pinned revision, download script, processing recipe, license filters, and training stages. The complementary swiss-ai/apertus-data-transparency repository records source versions
and licenses across all modalities. We keep raw, processed, and tokenized layers separate
to make the transformations between them explicit and repeatable. Download adapters
handle repository snapshots, archives, object stores, public APIs, and URL-based image
datasets. Processing decodes media, removes invalid records, checks minimum resolution,
normalizes schemas, encodes media in standard formats, and converts records to Arrow
or WebDataset. Dataset-specific adapters additionally handle large tiled geospatial images,
multi-image conversations, spatial annotations, and interleaved documents. When the in2 We provide code and benchmarks at swiss-ai/benchmark-audio-tokenizer.

11

ventory lists no additional source-specific operation, we apply these shared checks without
changing the publisher’s filtering decisions.
Deduplication. For unpaired continued-pretraining images, we deduplicate samples
with perceptual hashes and choose thresholds by domain. Most sources use exact equality
of 64-bit hashes after reduction to 32 × 32 pixels. Satellite images use a smaller hash input
to suppress repeated forest, water, and cloud tiles, while egocentric video frames allow a
small Hamming distance to remove adjacent near-duplicates. These rules are inappropriate for text-rich images such as documents and web interfaces, where distinct pages can
share a layout and produce the same hash. In early inspections of document and MINT-1T
runs, perceptual hashing removed valid OCR examples. We therefore disabled it for later
datasets centered on OCR and document understanding.
Caption Generation and Validation. For paired continued-pretraining data, we retain
informative source captions and regenerate missing, repetitive, or low-information descriptions for selected sources with open-weight vision-language models. The generation
pipelines reject empty outputs and apply source-specific output checks. For long captions
of text-dense technical drawings, we additionally detect token loops, runaway number
lists, and repeated endings caused by repeated labels and tabular schedules. We quarantine and regenerate flagged captions. These automated checks catch common generation
failures, but synthetic captions can still contain factual errors. The public recaptioning
pipelines and quality-control tools provide the shared generation and validation infrastructure.3
Processing at Scale. Preparing these corpora on shared supercomputing infrastructure
introduces constraints beyond data quality: unavailable source URLs, storage and inode
limits, scratch-retention policies, and network restrictions on bulk downloads. The raw
MINT-1T snapshot alone occupies approximately 700 TB, so we compress it early and apply successive filters in place. URL-based datasets create a different problem: collecting
the images generates crawl-like traffic across many domains, including destinations on security blocklists. Retries against denied endpoints put the Alps supercomputer’s shared
outbound IP at risk of blocking, with consequences for unrelated scientific workloads that
depend on the same network access. Multimodal data preparation therefore requires coordination with the center’s storage, network, and security teams to protect shared infrastructure while sustaining ingestion at scale. We describe the operational incidents and the
layered proxy that enforces network safeguards in Section 6.4.
2.2.2

V ISION T RAINING D ATA

Vision data enters all stages of pretraining and supervised finetuning. The tables in Appendix B give the vision pool of each stage, with samples and input tokens per dataset,
complementing the selection and preparation methodology in Section 3.1.
Across all six stages, the vision pools for Apertus 1.5 8B contain 821.6 million samples.
Most belong to the unpaired and paired stages of continued pretraining (Stages 1 and 2).
We report the share of vision data within each continued-pretraining stage’s token budget
separately in Table 3.
Stages 1 and 2 together contain approximately 86% of the vision samples across the six 8B
stage pools. Their scale explains why the overall composition in Figure 3 mostly reflects
these early stages, despite the different emphasis of later pools.

A UDIO T RAINING D ATA

Audio data enters the same six stages. Stage 1 uses unpaired speech, music, and mixed
audio, while the later stages concentrate on paired speech and text. Paired speech takes
three forms: transcription pairs, translation pairs, and interleaved sequences that alternate
the audio and transcripts of consecutive clips from one recording, with dedicated tokens
marking each switch between speech and text. Interleaved sequences make up about a fifth
of the Stage 2 and cooldown audio tokens and all of the long-context audio pool (Table C.3).
The tables in Appendix C give the audio pool of each stage, with samples and input tokens
per dataset.
Unlike vision, where we classify samples and weight categories by sample count, we group
entire audio datasets into six categories by source and purpose. Figure 5 shows their contributions by input tokens.
Web and broadcast speech dominate every pretraining pool. Read speech becomes more
prominent in later pretraining stages. Music and other audio remain small components.
The 70B Stage 2 and cooldown pools are identical. Dialogue and question answering dominate both SFT pools, with domain-specific speech (such as medical QA) contributing a
smaller component.

Interleaved Speech and Text. For the paired stages, we build interleaved sequences
from transcribed speech corpora such as VoxPopuli, Multilingual LibriSpeech, and WenetSpeech. We group the transcribed clips of each recording into runs of consecutive clips.
Within a run, a sequence alternates between modalities clip by clip: the audio of one
clip is followed by a speech-to-text continuation token and the transcript of the next clip,
which is followed by a text-to-speech continuation token and the audio of the clip after that, and every sequence ends with text. We build two copies of each run that start
one clip apart, so that a clip that appears as audio in one copy appears as text in the
other. Single-clip runs and the leftover clip of a run become transcription sequences
that pair one clip’s audio with its own transcript. Sequences of up to 8,192 tokens enter Stage 2, and longer ones enter the long-context extension. The builder is released in
swiss-ai/benchmark-audio-tokenizer.
14

Speech SFT Data. The auxiliary checkpoint used for capability filtering of text data (Section 4.2) and the production SFT runs draw from the same prepared audio pool of six
speech datasets (Table C.4). This pool contains speech inputs with text outputs and no
music or environmental-sound supervision. Its largest component by sample count renders roughly 640,000 conversations from eight open chat corpora (Lian et al., 2023; Zhao
et al., 2024; Ding et al., 2023; Singh et al., 2024; Durbin, 2023; Köpf et al., 2023), among
them Capybara and Pure-Dove (Daniele & Suphavadeeprasit, 2023), to speech with textto-speech synthesis, so that the assistant behavior of the text mixture carries over to spoken
instructions. For this component, we synthesize user turns with Qwen3-TTS (Hu et al.,
2026), while retaining assistant responses as text. We align the generated speech to the
corresponding source text using Qwen3-ForcedAligner (Shi et al., 2026). During dataset
construction, we exclude conversations with unavailable audio and verify that each audio placeholder corresponds to an associated audio segment. The remainder combines a
voice-assistant corpus of roughly 242,000 conversations (Xie & Wu, 2024), spoken question
answering with both machine-read and human-read questions (Wu et al., 2023), a frauddetection dialogue set (Ma et al., 2025), and a bilingual medical set that mixes Chinese
dialogues with English audio question answering and speech recognition (Chen et al.,
2025c). We process each dataset separately: we decontaminate the vocalized conversations against the evaluation benchmarks, removing 2,153 conversations, and filter them
to the languages supported by the synthesis pipeline, removing a further 6,141 conversations; we drop 4,306 identity-related rows from the voice-assistant corpus to protect the
model’s self-description; and we filter the medical set to its bimodal records (dropping
32,320 image-bearing records) and repair the missing audio placeholders.
Long-Context SFT Data. The long-context SFT pool in Table C.5 extends the ordinary SFT
pool with Marco Longspeech and AudioMCQ GeminiCoT, as well as additional TeleAntiFraud and Vocalized SFT samples. Marco Longspeech is its largest component by token
count. AudioMCQ GeminiCoT contributes only the speech partition of its source, with its
reasoning traces placed in the thinking span, and enters the short-context bucket of both
models’ long-context SFT runs. The pool inventory gives the available data; Table 8 reports
the per-source counts used by each model.
2.3

T OKENIZATION AND T RAINING P IPELINES

Megatron-LM (Shoeybi et al., 2019) natively supports multimodality, with two limitations.
First, it supports only continuous features: neither the multimodal models of Megatron
Core nor the sample types of Megatron Energon carry discrete tokens (Patel et al., 2025).
Second, it computes everything inside a training job: vision and audio encoders and a
projection layer embed pixels and waveforms in the forward pass, and the supervised
finetuning paths tokenize conversations and derive the loss mask from the chat template
at load time. This adds encoder compute and media decoding to every step, and it leaves
no entry point for pre-tokenized multimodal sequences, in pretraining or in supervised
finetuning.
Following prior work on incorporating discrete multimodal representations into pretrained LLMs (Ge et al., 2024; Zhan et al., 2024), we extend the base LLM by adding dedicated vocabulary ranges for vision and audio tokens. Each new token is assigned a corresponding embedding, allowing tokenized media to be consumed by the transformer in
the same way as text tokens. The vocabulary is extended correspondingly, and the output
projection is extended to produce logits for the newly introduced vision and audio tokens
in addition to the original text vocabulary. The newly introduced parameters are randomly
initialized, while all parameters inherited from the pretrained LLM are retained. Thus, the
transformer itself requires no modality-specific computation: multimodal sequences can
be processed with the standard autoregressive next-token prediction objective.
We therefore build two high-throughput tokenization pipelines from scratch, one for images and one for audio, that write Megatron indexed datasets directly. Section 2.2.1 describes the upstream acquisition and processing that feed them. The pipelines convert
each image or waveform into token ids from the corresponding added vocabulary range.
15

This is the payoff of discrete tokens: a codec maps an image or a waveform to ids from
a contiguous block of the vocabulary, so to the model a tokenized image is a text document in a language it has not seen before. The multimodal shards enter the training data
mixture through the standard data blending and sampling of Megatron-LM, and stream
through the unmodified pretraining data loader. Hence, continued pretraining can run on
an almost vanilla Megatron-LM installation.
The necessary changes to Megatron-LM live in our fork (swiss-ai/Megatron-LM). For
pretraining, we add options for masking multimodal tokens from prediction, which enables the text-only targets from the second training stage onward (Figure 1), and for exempting the multimodal special tokens from Goldfish-loss dropping (Section 3.2). For supervised finetuning, we add the missing entry point: a loader that reads pre-tokenized
conversations from the same indexed format and derives the loss mask efficiently on the
fly at the token level, masking user turns and tool responses without a separate preprocessing step or added overhead in the training step.
2.3.1

I MAGE T OKENIZATION

The pipeline, released in swiss-ai/benchmark-image-tokenizer, separates indexing, planning, and tokenization. A scan reads only tar headers and the first few kilobytes
of each image, recording byte offsets and dimensions for hundreds of millions of samples
in minutes, and never touches pixels. From the manifest a deterministic plan assigns every image to a GPU batch: images are grouped in windows of consecutive source rows,
snapped to document boundaries, sorted by their exact post-resize shape, and packed to
a token budget, so that every batch is shape-uniform and byte-adjacent, the ranks never
communicate, and the measured rank imbalance stays below 2% at 800 GPUs. Planning
is NumPy-only and costs seconds, so re-planning for a different batch size or GPU count
never requires a rescan. On each rank, loader threads decode, filter, and resize images
while the GPU encodes the previous batch, and text tokenization and chat-template rendering run concurrently with the encoder, which keeps the encoder at roughly 92% of wall
time. Each rank writes its own Megatron indexed-dataset shards and checkpoints after every batch, so an interrupted job resumes from its last written batch, and a final step merges
the shards; for SFT data, the merge can also write a copy with the reasoning spans removed. Two changes to the encoder itself (selecting codes by argmax rather than through
a 131,072-way softmax and one-hot product, and pinning TF32 as an explicit precision
contract), together with host-side overlap, roughly double throughput without changing a
single emitted token, whereas bf16 would flip 7% of codes and we therefore reject it.
2.3.2

A UDIO T OKENIZATION

Audio tokenization, released in swiss-ai/benchmark-audio-tokenizer, combines
the main systems challenges of text and image processing. Text inputs are variable-length,
but tokenization is CPU-bound and scales across many cores. Image tokenization is GPUbound, but resizing enables shape-homogeneous batches with high accelerator utilization.
Audio is both GPU-bound and variable-length, making efficient batching difficult without
excessive padding or underutilization. The pipeline first converts every source into Lhotse
Shar shards (Żelasko et al., 2021) and tokenizes any paired text during that conversion, so
the GPU stage handles only audio. Each rank receives its own set of whole shards and
never communicates with the others, and a dynamic bucketing sampler groups clips of
similar duration into batches capped by total audio duration while CPU workers decode
the next batch. Because batched encoding pads each clip to the longest in its batch and the
last few codes before the padding are unstable, the pipeline drops the last five codes after
padding.
2.4

A BLATIONS

Introducing new modalities into a pretrained language model can degrade its text capabilities, particularly during continued pretraining. Lu et al. (2024) report catastrophic forgetting of language capabilities during joint vision-language pretraining and retain at least
16

70% text data to mitigate it. Similarly, Kimi Team et al. (2026) observe an immediate drop
in text performance when vision data is introduced to a text-pretrained model, followed
by only gradual recovery during subsequent training.
Preserving the language capabilities of the base model is therefore a primary constraint
throughout the development of Apertus 1.5, alongside acquiring strong multimodal capabilities. This section studies the design choices of the continued pretraining that bear on
this constraint; Section 4.7 turns to post-training, where multimodal data interacts with
reasoning supervision.
Two design decisions of the continued pretraining rest on dedicated experiments: the
choice of the image tokenizer, which trades reconstruction quality for token efficiency, and
the two-stage recipe, which separates representation learning on unpaired images from
modality alignment on paired data.
Image Tokenizer. Irmiger et al. (2026) find that the tokenizers of the Emu family offer the
best trade-off between reconstruction quality and token efficiency (Section 2.1.1). Within
that family, we choose the tokenizer of Emu3.5 (Cui et al., 2025b) over that of Emu3 (Wang
et al., 2026) for its token efficiency. Emu3 encodes one code per 8 × 8 pixel patch and
Emu3.5 one per 16 × 16 patch, so Emu3.5 represents the same image with a quarter of the
tokens.
To quantify what this compression costs on our data, we benchmark both tokenizers on
a stratified sample of the Stage 1 vision mixture, built on the sample-level categories of
Section 2.2.3. We draw 50 images per category with a fixed seed and report a representative subset of six categories, 350 image pairs, that spans natural photographs, synthetic
imagery, structured graphics, text, and the two specialist domains. We omit the categories
not listed and merge charts and diagrams into one category, as in the composition figures.
We resize each image as we do for training Apertus, encode and decode it with each tokenizer, and score the reconstruction against the input with the peak signal-to-noise ratio
(PSNR), the structural similarity index (SSIM), and the learned perceptual image patch
similarity (LPIPS) (Table 1 and Figure 6).
Emu3.5 uses a quarter of the tokens of Emu3 in every category, at a cost of 2.7 dB PSNR,
0.07 SSIM, and 0.02 LPIPS averaged over the subset. The gap is consistent across categories:
Emu3 reconstructs better on every metric except PSNR on geospatial imagery, where the
two are almost level, and the largest PSNR losses fall on charts and diagrams, medical images, and documents, each between 3 and 4 dB. Medical images and documents are the
two categories in which Emu3.5 reconstructs worst in PSNR, which confirms our qualitative observation that fine-grained detail, and text in particular, is its main weakness. We
accept this trade: because image tokens typically dominate the token count of an image
sample, the fixed token budget of each pretraining stage covers approximately four times
as many images, and inference on image inputs is correspondingly cheaper. Although our
models see more images during training, the coarser grid is the likely reason that OCR
remains the capability group in which they trail other open models (Table 34).
Table 1: Reconstruction quality and token cost of the Emu3 and Emu3.5 tokenizers, averaged over 350 Stage 1 training images from six representative categories of Section 2.2.3
and preprocessed as in training. Tokens are the mean per image, arrows give the better
direction, and bold marks the better tokenizer; Table E.1 in Appendix E lists the values per

Two-Stage Recipe. A controlled study underpins the design of the first two pretraining
stages of Section 3.2 (Kreft, 2026). The study extends Llama 3.2 3B with the same Emu3.5
tokenizer and splits the topic-balanced LLaVA-OneVision 1.5 image-caption corpus (An
et al., 2025) in half: one half, stripped to standalone images, feeds a representation-learning

Figure 6: Per-category reconstruction quality of the Emu3 and Emu3.5 tokenizers on the

stage that predicts image tokens, mirroring our Stage 1; the other half, kept as imagecaption pairs, feeds a subsequent modality-alignment stage, mirroring our Stage 2. Both
halves share one distribution, and every batch replays 10% text-only data. Three findings
carry over. First, giving image and text tokens equal loss weight during modality alignment is harmful: this setting scores far below all others on nearly every group of imageunderstanding benchmarks, showing that image-token prediction and modality alignment
interfere when they share compute. Second, zero weight on image tokens during modality
alignment is the strongest single setting for image understanding, best on the documentunderstanding, grounding, and hallucination benchmark groups, while variants that end
the modality-alignment stage at zero weight stay competitive on the remaining groups;
this backs our text-only Stage 2 loss targets. Third, the representation-learning stage pays
off downstream: with the modality-alignment stage fixed, raising its budget from zero
to roughly 57B image tokens lifts image understanding measured after modality alignment and SFT, for example GQA from 0.50 to 0.54, DocVQA from 0.67 to 0.71, and SEEDBench from 0.61 to 0.65, with logarithmic gains that flatten at larger budgets; this backs
our unpaired all-token Stage 1 and its bounded token budget. At the largest budgets, however, knowledge-heavy image benchmarks and text benchmarks erode, as both rely on the
model’s language capabilities; the deficits persist despite the replayed text and do not fully
recover in the later stages, motivating the substantially larger text share of our mixtures
(Table 3).

18

3

C ONTINUED P RETRAINING R ECIPE

Apertus 1.5 is obtained through continued pretraining of Apertus 1.0, with the goal of
introducing multimodal capabilities, including image and audio, while preserving the text
capabilities of Apertus 1.0. We start from the Apertus 1.0 8B and 70B checkpoints and
proceed through four stages of continued pretraining (Figure 1): unpaired multimodal
training, paired multimodal training, and cooldown with higher-quality data, all at 8K
sequence length, followed by long-context extension to support context lengths up to 262K.
This chapter gives an overview of the continued-pretraining data and recipe. Section 3.1
describes the revised data mixture including source selection, data processing, and provenance requirements. Section 3.2 specifies the optimizer, learning-rate schedule, prediction
targets, and model-specific token budgets. Section 3.3 describes the long-context extension
and its data mixture. Finally, Section 3.4 examines the training dynamics and lessons from
the production runs.
3.1

P RETRAINING D ATA

We continue training on a new mixture based on the Apertus 1.0 text mixture, refined
with updated and additional text datasets and extended with image and audio data. We
use only publicly available datasets and document their provenance and filtering. This
subsection describes the data governance requirements shared across modalities, including compliance, provenance, and opt-out handling (Section 3.1.1), followed by the selection and refinement of the text mixture (Section 3.1.2). We maintain the source versions
and licenses for the full mixture in swiss-ai/apertus-data-transparency and the
source-specific vision and audio processing recipes in swiss-ai/multimodal-data.
We introduce multimodality through the discrete-token early-fusion approach presented
in Section 2.1: images and audio as discrete tokens that the model processes alongside
text in a shared token space. These new token spaces motivate the staged recipe shown
in Figure 1. In Stage 1, we combine text with unpaired images and audio and train the
model to predict tokens of all modalities. In Stage 2, we introduce paired image-text and
audio-text sequences and restrict prediction targets to text, aligning the new modalities
with language. The cooldown then emphasizes higher-quality and targeted sources, and
the long-context extension adds longer and interleaved records. Section 3.2 specifies the
model-specific token budgets, sampling ratios, and optimization schedule.
Details of the multimodal data recipe appear in Section 2, which describes the multimodal
design, the rationale for the staged training recipe (Section 2.4), and data preparation (Section 2.2). Section 2.2.2 and Section 2.2.4 describe the vision and audio data, respectively,
while Appendix B and Appendix C list the prepared pools across stages. For multimodal
sources, we apply the acquisition, filtering, deduplication, and caption validation procedures described in Section 2.2.1.
3.1.1

C OMPLIANCE AND P ROVENANCE

Every new modality is also required to satisfy the same requirements as the text
data in Apertus 1.0 (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026). Our review covers the upstream
source, revision, declared license, constituent datasets, and sample-level provenance
where available. For composite medical and remote-sensing collections, we select approved constituent sources or records rather than relying on the aggregate license. Noncommercial, research-only, and share-alike subsets are excluded, and the spreadsheet in
swiss-ai/apertus-data-transparency remains the authoritative record of the final decisions. No paywalled or otherwise closed sources are used for training. The text
pipeline additionally removes personally identifiable information and applies the multilingual toxicity filters of Apertus 1.0.
We apply machine-readable opt-outs (robots.txt files) following the same recipe as Apertus 1.0, and we honor robots exclusions retroactively: if a page owner opts out this year,
previous crawls of the same URL are excluded as well. The opt-out filtering also applies to
19

multimodal content when a record retains the page URL on which the content appeared.
For example, MINT-1T HTML preserves original page URLs, so we apply the same domain exclusion list as the text pipeline. For images, a strict allow-list limited to domains
represented in the FineWeb robots.txt database would discard roughly 96% of MINT-1T,
whereas the exclusion rule used by the text pipeline removes roughly 2%. For datasets that
retain only an image-hosting URL or the name of another dataset, we record that domainlevel opt-out filtering is not technically available, rather than treating the hosting service
as the content owner.
We materialize URL-based datasets by downloading original images from source URLs on
the shared Alps supercomputer at CSCS, and therefore separately apply network safety filtering to protect the center’s shared outbound network infrastructure. Section 6.4 describes
the proxy and blocklists. This safeguard is distinct from license review, opt-out handling,
and image content review; it does not constitute a comprehensive scan for sexual or violent content. For image and audio content, we otherwise retain the filtering supplied by
the dataset publishers.
3.1.2

T EXT D ATA S ELECTION

We refine the text mixture of Apertus 1.0 by replacing FineWeb2-HQ with FineWeb2HQ+ (Turki et al., 2026) and adding FinePDFs, Nemotron-CC-v2.1, SYNTH, FineTranslations, Nemotron-Pretraining-Code-v1, and legal-domain corpora. Table 4 reports the perstage sampling proportions of the resulting text mixture.
Every added source must be publicly available, pass license and provenance review, and
improve on the Apertus 1.0 mixture in a small-scale ablation. The baseline experiment
trains for 40,000 steps on FineWeb followed by a 10,000-step cooldown on FineWeb-Edu.
A candidate source replaces 30% of the cooldown data, and we evaluate it on the standard
development suite.4 For specialized sources, such as legal or medical data, we require an
improvement on the corresponding domain evaluations without a material regression on
the general suite. We also prefer sources that retain original URLs and other provenance
fields needed for later filtering.
For multilingual data, we extend the FineWeb2-HQ dataset (Messmer et al., 2025) used for
Apertus 1.0 to an improved variant, FineWeb2-HQ+ (Turki et al., 2026). The dataset retains
the 10% of FineWeb-2 documents per language rated highest by a multilingual quality
classifier, covering the 100 highest-resource languages and a 10% sample of the remaining
languages (originally, FineWeb2-HQ’s quality filter covered only 20 languages). The classifier recipe changes in two ways. First, we train a single classifier jointly on all languages
from a pool of positive samples widened with multilingual instruction data (including
MURI-IT, Tagengo, and WikiQA), with lower-resource languages upsampled by at most
a factor of three so that high-resource languages do not dominate the decision boundary.
Second, its negative samples are drawn from the 50th–75th percentile of a proxy quality
classifier rather than uniformly from FineWeb-2, which sets the boundary against fluent
but low-utility text instead of obvious noise (Turki et al., 2026).
3.2

O PTIMIZER AND T RAINING R ECIPE

We continue pretraining both Apertus 1.5 models, the 8B and 70B, with the AdEMAMix optimizer (Pagliardini et al., 2025) from Apertus 1.0 (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026), retaining
the same momentum hyperparameters. We resume from the Apertus 1.0 checkpoints that
have completed cooldown and use a warmup-stable-decay schedule: both models warm
up from zero to their peak learning rates over 4.2B tokens; the learning rate then stays at
its peak through the first two stages and decays to 10% of the peak with a 1 − sqrt decay
over the third. The 8B model keeps the peak learning rate of Apertus 1.0 8B, 1.1 × 10−4 .
For the 70B model, we double the peak of Apertus 1.0 70B to 2 × 10−5 , which outperforms
the original value in ablations, suggesting that the Apertus 1.0 70B peak learning rate was
4 The data ablation results are published at https://api.wandb.ai/links/bmessmer/
2l4dpwl1.

20

likely too small. To accommodate multimodal data, we double the sequence length from
4,096 tokens to 8,192 tokens. An image of 512 × 512 pixels is tokenized into 1,024 tokens
and a minute of audio into 2,400 tokens (Section 2.1.1, Section 2.1.2); multimodal sequences
interleaved with text documents routinely exceed the previous 4,096-token limit, and the
8,192-token window ensures that entire documents fit within a single sequence. We halve
the global batch size so that the number of tokens per batch remains the same as in Apertus
1.0, and reuse the same tensor and pipeline parallelism configurations to train Apertus 1.5.
Table G.1 summarizes the resulting configuration of both models.
We apply weight decay to all trainable parameters, including normalization gains and the
trainable parameters of the xIELU activation (Huang & Schlag, 2024), which we find helps
reduce large activation and activation-gradient norms. We initialize the optimizer states
from scratch, since the newly added input and output embeddings for image and audio
tokens have no existing optimizer states. The corresponding embedding and output rows
for the new tokens are randomly initialized, while the text rows keep their pretrained values; the fresh rows start at roughly a tenth of the norm of the trained text rows and adapt
over the first pretraining stage. For the 70B model, we also evaluate splicing in the multimodal embeddings learned by the 8B model, but we observe that it destabilizes training,
so we use random initialization instead. In the released checkpoints, we prune the output rows for image, audio, and reserved tokens from the language-model head, since the
models never generate non-text tokens and the text logits are unchanged.
We keep the Goldfish loss of Apertus 1.0 with its parameters unchanged: it drops the prediction target of a random subset of positions, thereby preventing verbatim memorization
of the training data and respecting copyright (Hans et al., 2024; Xu, 2025). We extend this
dropping to cover the predicted tokens of every modality; only special tokens, among them
the markers that structure image and audio sequences, are exempt. Image and audio tokens are prediction targets in the first stage alone. The later stages predict text only, which
prevents the model from generating non-text tokens. From the second stage, we likewise
exclude from prediction the task-control markers that select between transcription and continuation: they condition the model but are never targets.
Before the long-context extension, continued pretraining proceeds in three stages: unpaired
multimodal training, paired multimodal training, and cooldown with higher-quality data.
The stages are summarized in Table 2 and Table 3, and the text data mix in Table 4; the
vision and audio datasets of every stage are listed in Appendix B and Appendix C. Each
stage’s dataset list is frozen before the stage begins: the deterministic sampling plan depends on the full list, and mid-stage changes would shift that plan, silently repeating or
skipping documents. In the first stage we train on unpaired image, audio, and text data,
with the loss computed over tokens of all modalities. In the second and third stages we
add the respective parallel data (image-text and audio-transcript pairs) along with textonly data, and compute the loss over the text tokens only, since the models understand
but never generate images and audio (Section 2); an ablation further shows that text-only
targets during paired training also yield the strongest image understanding (Section 2.4).
For the 8B, the Stage 2 text share rises to roughly two thirds, in part because multimodal
data was still limited; the 70B, with its halved token budget, keeps a roughly even split
between text and multimodal data, yet its text evaluations do not degrade. The third stage
is the cooldown, in which we emphasize higher-quality data and include additional paired
speech sources (Table C.2). The audio portion of the cooldown accounts for 95B input tokens for Apertus 1.5 8B and 92B for Apertus 1.5 70B (Table 3). These training counts include
paired text and any repeated sampling; they do not measure unique hours of speech. The
cooldown also includes a small share of instruction-formatted data (roughly 3B tokens), for
which the loss is restricted to assistant tokens. We train the 70B model on half the intended
number of tokens to free resources for the next Apertus pretraining project, keeping the
cooldown length unchanged and reducing the token budget mainly during Stage 2.

3.3

L ONG -C ONTEXT E XTENSION

The long-context extension is the fourth and last stage of continued pretraining (Figure 1),
and follows the three stages of Section 3.2. We extend the context window in four steps,
from 8,192 to 32,768, 65,536, 131,072, and finally 262,144 tokens. Each step starts from the
checkpoint produced by the previous one and reinitializes the optimizer states. Depending
on the phase and model size, we train on 50B–63B tokens per step.
We keep the learning rate fixed at the final value reached during cooldown. This provides a
conservative continuation of training after cooldown, limiting changes to the model while
adapting it to longer sequences. We also include synthetic long-context tasks to explicitly
train capabilities that require using information over long distances. Whenever we double
the sequence length, we halve the number of sequences per batch so that the total number
of tokens per batch remains constant.
We also adjust the rotary position embeddings as the context window grows. The main
runs initially keep the base frequency of 500,000 and scaling factor of 8 used in Apertus
1.0. By the end of the four extension stages, the final model uses a base frequency of
4M and a scaling factor of 32. Documents are packed using a best-fit-decreasing strategy (Ding et al., 2024). Cross-document attention masking under context parallelism also
makes training substantially cheaper: training at 131,072 tokens costs roughly the same as
training at 65,536 tokens did for Apertus 1.0.
The prepared data for each step keeps the same relative composition regardless of phase
or model size: most tokens come from a subsample of the cooldown mixture, and the remainder splits into genuinely long documents and synthetic verifiable long-context tasks.
Because each step trains on fewer tokens than this prepared pool, Appendix F gives the
exact, already-scaled per-dataset, per-step token counts and sources actually used in training. As throughout pretraining, we preprocess and filter every dataset before use. The long
documents, split into English text, multilingual text, and multimodal image-text and audio
corpora, are length-filtered subsets of similar pretraining sources used earlier in the recipe;
we draw them proportionally to each source’s own size so that no single source dominates
a step. Table 5 gives the token budget and within-phase share of each class.
Each step targets a fixed image budget, but the natively length-filtered image pool cannot
supply enough tokens at the 32k, 65k, and 131k phases, since fewer real documents in the
pretraining corpora happen to span exactly those lengths; only the 262k phase has enough
native long-context images on its own. We close the gap at the shorter phases by drawing
additional images from short-context documents (1,000–16,384 tokens) so that every phase
reaches the same image budget: native long-context images make up about 79% of the 32k
budget, 47% of the 65k budget, and 34% of the 131k budget, with the short-context top-up
filling the rest. Table B.4 gives further per-dataset sample counts for the vision pool.
The remaining tokens per step are synthetic verifiable long-context tasks from
swiss-ai/LongContextQA: we inject Q&A turns into a long document and re-tokenize
it as one training example. Common-word extraction (CWE) (Team Olmo et al., 2025)
and document-order reconstruction (DocOrder) together contribute most of these tokens:
CWE asks how many times each of several sampled words occurs in the document, and
DocOrder shuffles a document’s sections and asks for their original order, each with 10
randomized question or answer formats so the model cannot rely on one fixed phrasing.
Both tasks scale in difficulty with the packed length by varying how densely they quiz
each document: the hard bucket draws shorter source documents and packs more items
into them (10 questions for CWE, eight sections for DocOrder), so the model must track
more items with less surrounding context; the easy bucket draws longer source documents
and packs fewer items into them (five questions, four sections), testing the same skill with
more context to spare. Mixing both buckets balances signal across the full range of packed
lengths. Artificial needles (Xiong et al., 2025) add the rest, planting a retrievable fact among
synthetic distractor listings. We add masking in the input sequence to avoid biasing the
models toward incorrect or random long context in DocOrder and Artificial needles. The
SFT token counts reported for these tasks count the whole packed example, including the

3.4

P RETRAINING R UN R ETROSPECTIVE

We discard the first Stage 1 run for the 8B and restart it. That run had two problems.
First, its peak learning rate was set to twice the Apertus 1.0 value, a choice that small-scale
ablations had favored but that did not transfer to this setting. Second, its text mixture
silently omitted the English quality-web, math, and code subsets: the corpus was spread
across personal scratch directories with no single manifest, so the launch scripts only saw
part of it. As a result, text capability degraded; the text loss eventually recovered, but
the evaluations did not. The restart uses the original peak learning rate and the corrected
mixture. We keep the text-to-multimodal ratio and accept a drop of about two points on
general text evaluations as the cost of adding the new modalities; text evaluations recover
by Stage 2. The 70B, by contrast, has enough capacity to absorb the new modalities with
visibly less text-capability cost than the 8B (the Stage 1 creep is far milder in Figure 7b than
in Figure 7a), and we choose its peak learning rate by continuing a pre-cooldown Apertus
1.0 checkpoint with preserved optimizer states on unchanged data, letting the loss settle,
and comparing peak learning rates from there. This gives a reliable signal within tens of
billions of tokens, and it selects twice the original peak. These two observations suggest
that the text-capability cost of adding modalities is capacity-dependent.
Ablations narrow down what drives the Stage 1 text-loss creep. Returning to the original
peak learning rate reduces the slow upward creep of the text loss during Stage 1 (top row,
Figure 7) but does not remove it; we rule out weight-decay settings as the cause; freezing
all non-embedding parameters at the start of training only delays the creep; and the creep
appears at mixed text-to-multimodal ratios but not near either extreme, consistent with
gradient interference between modalities (Hao et al., 2026). The main driver, however, is
the schedule itself: Apertus 1.0 ends cooled down, and warming the learning rate back to
its peak costs text performance until the next cooldown recovers it; both the Stage 1 rise
and its reversal over the Stage 3 cooldown are visible in the top row of Figure 7. The same
text-loss increase upon introducing multimodal data is reported for Kimi K2.5, which for
this reason keeps its learning-rate schedule unchanged (Kimi Team et al., 2026).
Figure 7 shows the loss of both runs across the three stages: the top row on text tokens only,
comparable across stages and exposing the Stage 1 creep and Stage 3 recovery discussed
above, and the bottom row over each stage’s own prediction targets.
5 Estimated from representative per-task answer lengths (CWE: about six tokens per turn; DocOrder: about 14 tokens for the single order-list answer; Artificial needles: about 25 tokens per turn)
and the number of samples per phase: the actually optimized share of SFT tokens is well under 1%,
from about 0.3% at 32k down to about 0.05% at 262k.

24

(a) Apertus 1.5 8B, text loss

(b) Apertus 1.5 70B, text loss

(c) Apertus 1.5 8B, combined loss

(d) Apertus 1.5 70B, combined loss

Figure 7: Per-stage loss against consumed tokens. Top (a, b): text loss, computed over text
tokens only in every stage and therefore comparable across stage boundaries. The text loss
creeps upward through Stage 1 and then drops sharply over the Stage 3 cooldown. Bottom
(c, d): combined loss over all of each stage’s prediction targets (Table 2): image, audio, and
text tokens in Stage 1, text only from Stage 2 onward. In Stage 1 the combined loss stays
stable, so the text-loss creep visible in the top row is masked when text and multimodal
targets are averaged together; the drop at the Stage 1→2 boundary reflects the change of
targets, not a change in quality. Averages are computed within each stage.

The 70B gains little over the 8B on multimodal tasks despite being nine times larger (Section 5.5 and Section 5.6). We see two causes. First, the 70B sees less multimodal data: its
run stops before consuming the full multimodal mixture, because its nodes are reallocated
to the next Apertus pretraining campaign. Since multimodal ability improves throughout
the mixture, this early stop costs the 70B much of the gain it would otherwise have made.
Second, the base learning rate of the AdEMAMix optimizer used to pretrain Apertus 70B
was likely too low; continued pretraining uses a learning rate proportional to the original,
so a low base rate leaves little room for the model to adapt to a new modality.
We also ablated adding a small share of instruction-formatted data during the cooldown.
This improved math, code, and instruction-following benchmarks. Replaying the
instruction-formatted data five-fold further improved benchmarks but regressed safety behavior due to overfitting on the small refusal subsets, so the small share performed better.
Both preference alignment and reinforcement learning improved long-context evaluations
even though neither stage trains on long documents, suggesting that gains on short contexts carry over to long ones.
We also encountered various infrastructure and tooling incidents. During the 70B longcontext extension, virtual pipelining halves throughput, so we disable it for that stage. Scaling the run to more nodes requires manually resharding optimizer states, as the memoryefficient resharding path fails at this size; we preserve the states rather than reinitializing
25

them mid-run, since reinitializing would break the resumed optimization trajectory. Near
the end of the 70B cooldown, some datasets are exhausted and the loss creeps slightly as
the effective mixture shifts: because we blend proportionally by size, exhausting smaller
datasets moves the mixture away from its target composition. The conversion used for
evaluations also silently drops the position-embedding scaling configuration, so converted
checkpoints underperform the checkpoints they come from, and part of the apparent regression is an artifact of conversion rather than the model.
Data tooling caused incidents as well. Because the corpus was assembled by a distributed
effort across the team, some datasets were not stored on persistent storage and fell under
automatic deletion policies on the Alps cluster; parts of the tokenized corpus were deleted
mid-project. The data loss was recoverable, because every dataset could be regenerated
from its preparation script. A bug in the decontamination script removed an entire dataset
from the mixture over a single flagged sample; we caught it before training. Checkpoint inspection also exposes upstream data artifacts that aggregate validation checks miss: some
captions in the upstream BLIP3 Grounding 50M dataset retain fragments of the recaptioning prompt and follow a single repeated response template, and the pretrained model occasionally reproduces those instructions or applies the template to unrelated images. This
motivates direct inspection of generated samples and explicit prompt-fragment checks in
addition to schema and aggregate quality checks.

26

4

P OST-T RAINING

Post-training proceeds in five stages (Figure 1): supervised finetuning (SFT), long-context
SFT, reinforcement learning (RL), preference alignment with DPO, and charter alignment
with SDPO. Every stage trains on conversations in the same format, which we therefore
describe first.
4.1

C HAT T EMPLATE

The conversation format of Apertus 1.0 carries over: special tokens delimit system, developer, user, and assistant messages, and the developer message declares the tools available
to the model (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026). Here we describe what Apertus 1.5 adds.
Two conventions are deliberate. The template renders an explicit empty system block when
a conversation has no system message, so no user-controlled content can occupy the system
slot. And the training conversations carry no knowledge-cutoff date, which keeps downstream finetunes free of a stale date. The released inference template differs in one respect:
when a conversation has no system message, it inserts a short default system prompt that
names the model and its modalities (Appendix K). Unlike the Apertus 1.0 default, this
prompt contains neither a knowledge cutoff nor the current date.
Images and audio enter user messages as the placeholder tokens <|image|> and
<|audio|>, which at tokenization time expand into the structure markers and content
codes of Section 2.1.1 and Section 2.1.2. The literals <image> and <audio> are aliases:
the tokenizer maps them to the same identifiers, so existing datasets that use these literals
tokenize correctly without conversion.
Tool outputs are enclosed by the tokens <|tool_output_start|> and
<|tool_output_end|>, new in this release, which repurpose two reserved identifiers of the Apertus 1.0 vocabulary. During training the delimiters identify tool outputs
so that they are excluded from the loss: the model conditions on them without learning
to generate them. Explicit tokens are necessary because the raw bracket characters that
previously enclosed tool results cannot be masked reliably at the token level; brackets
merge into hundreds of vocabulary tokens. The assistant emits tool calls as a JSON array
between <|tools_prefix|> and <|tools_suffix|>: each call is an object that maps
the tool name to its arguments, and parallel calls are elements of the same array. The
outputs of parallel calls return between the tool-output delimiters, separated by commas.
The tool declaration in the developer message is unchanged from Apertus 1.0: available
tools render as TypeScript-style type declarations, and a conversation without tools
declares them as disabled.
In thinking mode the model reasons between <|inner_prefix|> and
<|inner_suffix|> before its visible answer. Thinking is off by default and enabled per request through the chat template. The two delimiters are deliberately not
marked as special tokens, so they survive detokenization and downstream code can parse
the reasoning span out of the decoded text. The literals <think> and </think> alias
to the same identifiers. The two surface forms repeatedly confuse downstream tooling
during development, with training data, reinforcement-learning parsers, and serving
stacks latching onto different renderings; the token identifiers, not the rendered strings,
are canonical. The template itself supports tool calls inside the thinking span; that the
launch serving integrations expose tool calling and thinking only separately is a serving
limitation, not a format constraint. Figure 8 shows a rendered conversation that exercises
the full format: the default system prompt, the developer message with the deliberation
flag and a tool declaration, and an assistant turn that thinks, calls the tool, and answers
after the tool output returns.

4.2

S UPERVISED F INETUNING

We use SFT to develop assistant behavior over text, image, and speech inputs, with text
output, using the shared conversation format of Section 4.1. The mixture covers instruction following, multilingual interaction, reasoning, code, tool use, safety, and model identity. We train the models to provide reasoning traces when thinking is enabled and direct
answers when it is disabled.
To curate the text mixture, we train an auxiliary nonthinking checkpoint of Apertus 1.5 8B
from its long-context pretrained checkpoint (Section 3.3), using text, image, and audio supervision with reasoning traces removed. We use this checkpoint for capability filtering:
we evaluate selected verifiable text prompts and determine which receive verified reasoning traces.
For production SFT, we initialize Apertus 1.5 8B and Apertus 1.5 70B from their respective
long-context pretrained checkpoints and use the text mixture curated with the auxiliary
Apertus 1.5 8B checkpoint. Only the curated data carries over from the auxiliary run; neither production model inherits its weights. After production SFT, we continue training
both models with dedicated long-context SFT to address degraded long-context retrieval
(Section 4.3).
Both production SFT runs use a constant learning rate after 800 warmup iterations and a
sequence length of 16,384 tokens: Apertus 1.5 8B trains at 6 × 10−5 with a global batch size
of 256 for roughly 159B tokens, and Apertus 1.5 70B at 1 × 10−5 with a global batch size of
1,024 for roughly 155B tokens.6 The auxiliary capability-filtering run instead decays a peak
learning rate of 8 × 10−5 linearly, at a global batch size of 1,024.
Capability Filtering. As outlined in the introduction to Section 4.2, we train an auxiliary
nonthinking Apertus 1.5 8B checkpoint from its long-context pretrained checkpoint (Section 3.3), using text, image, and audio supervision with reasoning traces removed. We use
this checkpoint to sample eight nonthinking answers for each selected verifiable prompt
and check the final answers, extracted with the verifiable answer tool, against the reference. Prompts solved at least once (pass@8) remain in the mixture without a reasoning
trace; prompts that fail all eight attempts receive a verified reasoning trace. Figure 9 illustrates this procedure. Our ablations motivate this selective use of reasoning traces: with

We apply capability filtering to the single-turn subset of our instruction-following (IF)
mixture, OpenMathReasoning, DeepMath-103K, NuminaMath, and HARP. The share of
prompts that remain without reasoning traces varies substantially by source: 99% for
instruction following and roughly 10–40% for mathematics, with 11.9% for the 400kprompt OpenMathReasoning subset, 38.3% for DeepMath-103K, 22.7% for NuminaMath,
and 15.1% for HARP. We ablate k = 1, . . . , 8; for k below eight, the filtering rate on the
mathematics datasets is negligible, so we select pass@8 for the final run.
Data. The text component of the final production SFT mixture contains 3,707,851 samples
(Table 6). Relative to Apertus 1.0, it shifts weight toward instruction following, mathematics, and code, adds newer multilingual chat data, carries refusal-style safety data whose
responses we regenerate and verify as described below, and carries roughly 250,000 samples that reference the Apertus identity, mostly through their system prompts, though no
dedicated identity question-answer pairs. The reasoning traces come partly from openly
released datasets whose traces are distilled from open reasoning models, and partly from
traces we generate ourselves on Alps. For mathematics and instruction following, we generate verified reasoning traces with thinking enabled, using multiple models: Kimi-K2.6,
Qwen3.6-27B, Gemma-4-31B, and MiniMax-M2.7. We deduplicate text prompts, and for
the capability-filtering phase we filter out prompts that could lead to verifiable answers,
such as math prompts, using an LLM-as-a-judge (Kimi-K2.6). The text mixture remains
thin on long multi-turn conversations, with only roughly 30,000 samples of three or more
turns.
The training mixture for the auxiliary checkpoint that we subsequently use for capability filtering of text data includes an image pool of 20 datasets with roughly 58B tokens
and an audio pool of six datasets with roughly 1.1B tokens (Table 7, Table C.4). For this
checkpoint’s multimodal supervision, we follow a zero-reasoning policy: we regenerate
samples whose sources contain reasoning traces with Qwen3.6-27B and thinking disabled.
The final production SFT mixture uses the expanded image pool in Table B.5, including
reasoning-bearing additions described in Section 4.2.1, and the same prepared audio pool
(Table C.4).
Tool-Use Data. We select tool-use datasets by whether their trajectories are executionverified, rather than filtered only by an LLM judge, and drop candidates that do not meet
this bar. The mixture combines a curated subset of Toucan (Xu et al., 2026) (119,287 sam29

ples), EnvScaler (Song et al., 2026) (9,022), OpenSeeker (Du et al., 2026) (4,949), an
execution-verified subset of the Jupyter Agent dataset (Colle et al., 2025) (roughly 30,000
sandbox-verified notebooks), Glaive function calling (Glaive AI, 2023) (roughly 52,000),
and GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021) trajectories with calculator tool use (roughly 7,400). We
validate and clean the tool-use datasets to ensure correct conversation formatting, declared
tools, valid call arguments, and matching nonempty tool outputs. Because a model that
sees tools only in tool-calling samples never learns to ignore them, we additionally inject
tool declarations into 131,557 conversations that do not call any tool, teaching the model
to answer directly even when tools are available. A cleanup pass strips 950,544 calculatorannotation blocks that appear in samples without a corresponding tool declaration.
Multilingual and Safety Data. We use updated versions of EuroBlocks-SFT-2512 (Ramos
et al., 2026) and Llama-Nemotron Safety (Bercovich et al., 2025) to improve our safety
30

and multilingual capabilities. For Llama-Nemotron Safety, we observe over-refusals of
benign prompts and, on some occasions, harmful responses to dangerous prompts; therefore, we regenerate responses with GPT-OSS-120B-Safeguard and verify their validity
with a Qwen3-235B judge to improve assistant behavior and mitigate over-refusal. In
the EuroBlocks-SFT-2512 dataset, we observe that some of the assistant responses contain traces of the original EuroLLM identity training with the model name present in the
provided outputs. Since we cannot remove something like that verbatim, we use a pool
of several models (GPT-OSS-120B, MiniMax-M2, Qwen3.5-397B) randomly sampled to regenerate responses with the Apertus identity provided in the system prompt. Of the 1M
original samples, we use only 286k non-English conversations to diversify our multilingual
training mix.
Decontamination. We decontaminate the SFT mixture against a list of 595 evaluation
benchmarks with n-gram matching, and manually verify the flagged samples of every affected dataset rather than dropping hits blindly, since thresholds tuned for English text
over-trigger on multimodal boilerplate. The true positives concentrate in multilingual
datasets that contain translated benchmark items: in PangeaInstruct, roughly 71% of the
9,114 flagged samples are genuine benchmark translations, estimated from a manual inspection of 100 random hits. For EuroBlocks 2512, almost all of the removed samples are
unrelated non-contaminated multilingual samples. For our instruction-following mix, the
majority of “flagged” samples contain an instruction format similar to some from the IFEval benchmark, which does not necessarily mean direct contamination. We still decide to
keep the original similarity threshold of 0.5 (as used in Apertus 1.0) to ensure we catch
contaminated samples at the cost of false positives, which is still not critical. We remove
contaminated samples by identifier. For image data, text-based matching does not transfer
at all: templated prompts dominate, so nearly every n-gram hit is a false positive, and
matching must happen at the image level instead. We therefore match the perceptual
hashes of the training images (Section 2.2.1) against roughly 950,000 benchmark images;
this catches near-duplicates of natural images but produces false positives in low-entropy
domains such as satellite and medical imagery, which require manual thresholding. We
provide full details with counts of dropped samples for every dataset from the text SFT
mixture in Section H.2 (Table H.2).
4.2.1

I MAGE

The image pool used to train the auxiliary checkpoint for capability filtering of text data
spans 20 datasets with 17,534,672 sequences and roughly 58.1B tokens; Table 7 lists them.
We assemble the pool under the zero-reasoning policy of that checkpoint’s training: we
exclude or strip sources whose assistant turns carry reasoning markup, and for templateheavy sources we regenerate the assistant answers with Qwen3.6-27B and thinking disabled. The permissively licensed subset of LLaVA-OneVision-1.5 (An et al., 2025) and
SenseNova-SI-8M (Cai et al., 2026) together hold roughly 80% of the tokens in Table 7;
the remainder covers document, chart, and table understanding; captioning and open
question answering; GUI pointing; and domain imagery from remote sensing, pathology,
and memes. The production phase additionally trains on reasoning-bearing image data:
Innovator-VL (Wen et al., 2026) ships with the boundaries of its thinking spans stripped
by its creators, so we regenerate the reasoning traces by reformatting the samples with
Qwen3.6-27B and keep only outputs whose answers match the original with a ROUGEL score above 0.9; in the pool of Table B.5, roughly 2.7 million of the dataset’s 24.8 million
samples carry restored reasoning. We remove structured answer tags from source datasets,
so that all supervision matches the Apertus chat format; the processing of the image SFT
datasets otherwise follows the multimodal data pipeline of Section 2.2. The share of text
data packed into image-SFT batches matters little in ablations, with benchmark movements
small and split between 2.5% and 15%. For three FineVision subsets we regenerate the assistant turns with Qwen3.6-27B: for LNQA we keep the questions and replace the templatelike single-fact answers with image-grounded answers of two to four sentences, and for
emo-visual-data and Memotion 2 we replace the original Chinese descriptions and sentiment labels with two-turn English explanations of each meme, dropping samples that the
generator flags as unsafe. In the Apertus 1.5 8B production run, text makes up 13.6% of the
31

sequences and 7.8% of the tokens. Besides Innovator-VL, the production image pool carries thinking spans in Nemotron-Image-Training-v3 (22% of sequences), SenseNova-SI-8M
(5%), and RSRCC (all sequences).
The production candidate pool contains 57,130,672 sequences and 157.8B input tokens. After we create the training and development splits and complete final packing, the pool
of the Apertus 1.5 8B run contains 43,915,058 sequences and 143.4B input tokens, and Table B.5 gives its per-dataset composition.

4.2.2

A UDIO

The audio pool used to train the auxiliary checkpoint for capability filtering of text data
is speech only, with no music or environmental-sound supervision: six datasets with
1,116,511 sequences and roughly 1.1B tokens, all speech in and text out. We synthesize
and filter these datasets as described in Section 2.2.4. The production SFT runs draw from
the same six-dataset pool listed in Table C.4. These counts describe prepared data available
to the runs, rather than sampling weights, repetition factors, or tokens actually consumed.
The later long-context SFT stage draws from the extended pool in Table C.5; Table 8 gives
its per-model training counts.
4.2.3

VARIETY-AWARE T RANSLATION C ASE S TUDY

Generating text in the correct variety of a multi-variety language can be a challenge for language models, especially when some varieties are underrepresented in the training data.
Hernández-Cano et al. (2026) studied this problem for Romansh, a Swiss national language
with six distinct varieties. While Apertus 1.0 outperformed comparable models at translation from German into Romansh, it still struggled to adhere to the distinct varieties of the
language. We perform a case study to investigate how a small amount of variety-aware
post-training data affects this behavior.
We follow a back-translation approach (Sennrich et al., 2016). We translate a sample of
Romansh text into German with machine translation, and during supervised finetuning
we train Apertus to translate the German text back into the specific Romansh variety. The
monolingual Romansh text comes from the swiss-ai/apertus-pretrain-romansh
collection released with Apertus 1.0 (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026). We identify the variety of each text with the language identification system of Model et al. (2026), and translate into German with the machine translation system of Vamvas et al. (2026). We include 5,000 samples per variety in the finetuning mixture, and release the SFT dataset at
swiss-ai/apertus-pretrain-romansh-backtranslated-sft.
Table 32 reports the effect on translation from German into the six Romansh varieties, and
the human evaluation in Table 33 additionally covers German into Sursilvan.
33
