4.3

L ONG -C ONTEXT S UPERVISED F INETUNING

Preserving long-context retrieval through post-training is a known challenge: short-context
SFT systematically biases attention toward local patterns, degrading the long-range retrieval established during context extension (Zhou et al., 2026). After performing SFT at
16,384 tokens we confirm this behavior: the RULER (Hsieh et al., 2024) score at 262,144 tokens drops from 54.8 to 8.3 in Apertus 1.5 8B, while short-context scores remain unaffected.
We address this with a dedicated long-context SFT stage at the full sequence length of
262,144 tokens, following the staged recipe of Qwen 2.5 (Qwen et al., 2024) and Nemotron 3
Nano Omni (NVIDIA et al., 2026). Each model keeps its production SFT learning rate,
held constant: 6 × 10−5 for Apertus 1.5 8B and 1 × 10−5 for Apertus 1.5 70B, after 195 and
100 warmup steps and at global batch sizes of 32 and 64 sequences, respectively. Once this
34

stage finishes, we evaluate the resulting checkpoints across context lengths and select the
best one to carry forward into reinforcement learning.
Data. The mixture blends 30.3% long-context multimodal data, 63.2% short-context multimodal data, 5.4% short-context text data, and 1.0% long-context text data, weighted by
each source’s token count. The multimodal share is vision and audio, each split into shortand long-context length buckets. The text share replays a subset of the production SFT mixture (Table 6) at short context and reuses the synthetic verifiable tasks of Section 3.3 at long
context, and tool-use data enters at both lengths. Table 8 gives the per-source counts and
length ranges. The short-context audio subset draws from the ordinary SFT speech pool
(Table C.4) and AudioMCQ GeminiCoT, while the long-context audio rows include Marco
Longspeech and longer TeleAntiFraud and Vocalized SFT samples. The broader available
pool appears in Table C.5; its inventory does not imply that every listed source enters each
run.
4.4

I MPROVING I NSTRUCTION F OLLOWING

Instruction following is one of the target skills we aim to improve for Apertus 1.5. IF scores
improve over the course of post-training, as seen in Table 26 and Table 9.
Verifiable IF. We curate the verifiable IF SFT data through different synthetic data
pipelines; the data consists of the following subsets:
• Single-turn verified. We sample the prompts from the capability-filtering SFT
prompt pool and the Dolci Instruct SFT Pool (used for the OLMo 3 SFT run (Team
Olmo et al., 2025)), and concatenate them with constraints from IFTrain (Pyatkin
et al., 2025) and IFEval (Zhou et al., 2023). We either prepend or append the constraints to the instruction. We sample the completions from different open-weight
models, such as Qwen3-235B, Kimi-K2.5, MiniMax-M2.7, Qwen3.5-397B, and GPTOSS-120B-Safeguard. We then verify each prompt-completion pair twice: once for
whether it passes all the verifiers of the constraints in the prompt, and then with an
LLM-as-a-judge for whether the prompt and the constraints contradict each other
and whether the answer is coherent.
• Multi-turn verified. The multi-turn subset consists of multiple turns, where the first
user turn is an instruction specifying the constraint and saying that this constraint
should be followed throughout the whole conversation. The following user turns
are prompts, sampled from the capability-filtering SFT prompt pool and the Dolci
Instruct SFT Pool, with no constraints. We verify the completions similarly, by
checking whether they fulfill the initial constraint and whether they are coherent.
Improving Non-verifiable Instruction Following. In real-life user-AI conversations, instructions can be presented in a form which is hard to verify, e.g., “When talking about
table tennis, focus more on specific forehand serve technique of this player...”, which can result in
numerous correct responses, but the verification method would be based on an LLM-asa-judge rather than a classic IFEval-like hard-coded constraint check. Moreover, conversations in real life are long and multi-turn with instructions and preferences shifting middialogue. To target this area more, we construct a dataset with a multi-agent pipeline in
which three roles, such as a planner, a responder, and an evaluator, collaborate to generate
and validate each dialogue. The planner, given a seed context, a target challenge category,
and a one-shot example from MultiChallenge (Deshpande et al., 2025), produces a JSON
plan specifying the challenge type, an N-turn user question sequence, a hidden trap design,
and concrete detection criteria. A trap is a hidden test planted in the conversation (e.g., an
early instruction or detail the user later implicitly relies on) that the assistant fails (“triggers”) if it forgets or mishandles it, and the dataset keeps only dialogues in which the trap
is set up but not triggered. The responder simulates the assistant under test by following
the planned questions, and the evaluator inspects the full dialogue to verify that the trap
is present and whether it is triggered, and to score quality on a 10-point scale across safety,
35

accuracy, naturalness, and authenticity. We use seed contexts from Nemotron-InstructionFollowing-Chat-v1 (NVIDIA et al., 2025), MiniMax-M2.5 as a generator model (planning
and responding), and Qwen3-235B as a judge (evaluator) to mitigate model-specific bias.
For each seed we generate four plans (one per challenge category) and three trajectories per
plan, then retain only dialogues in which the trap is correctly introduced but not triggered
by the assistant, selecting the top-1 scored trajectory per seed (top-2 for eight-turn chats)
to balance quality and diversity. The generated 30,000 dialogues span one to eight conversation turns, with an average of three turns. The dataset shows improved performance
on tasks involving more vague constraints compared to standard IF benchmarks, while its
multi-turn nature assists with improving on Multi-IF.
Reinforcement Learning. We sample the IF RL prompts from the capability-filtering SFT
prompt pool and concatenate them with constraints from IFTrain (Pyatkin et al., 2025) and
IFEval (Zhou et al., 2023). We either prepend or append the constraints to the instruction.
Preference Alignment. Instruction following also profits from the preference stage of
Section 4.6, although no constraint checker is involved: prompts with formatting and content constraints are part of the Dolci-Instruct-DPO prompt set, and the judge scores each
rollout on how well it serves the request. To isolate this contribution, we apply online DPO
directly to the SFT checkpoint of Apertus 1.5 8B, skipping reinforcement learning, with the
recipe of Section 4.6 except for eight rollouts per prompt instead of 12 (Table 9). Judgebased preference optimization alone matches the verifier-driven reinforcement-learning
stage on IFEval and comes within two points of it on Multi-IF, even though instruction following is a verifiable domain. The gain is specific to on-policy training: offline DPO from
the same checkpoint leaves IFEval unchanged and adds roughly three points on Multi-IF.
The two stages are complementary rather than redundant, as online DPO on top of the RL
checkpoint adds a further 3.8 points on IFEval and 1.5 on Multi-IF. At 70B, the preference
stage keeps IFEval and Multi-IF within one point of the reinforcement-learning checkpoint
while restoring response length from 253 to 495 tokens and raising AlpacaEval from 25.9
to 59.4, so the added verbosity does not come at the expense of constraint adherence.
Table 9: Instruction following of Apertus 1.5 8B under different post-training paths from
the same SFT checkpoint, thinking disabled. The SFT column is the shared starting point,
the next two apply a single alignment stage to it, and the last two are the checkpoints of
the release lineage (Table 26). Best value per row in bold.

R EINFORCEMENT L EARNING

The reinforcement learning stage optimizes verifiable rewards with a fully asynchronous
trainer on our fork of verl (Sheng et al., 2025); Section 6.4 describes the infrastructure and
Appendix G the algorithmic configuration. Rewards come from checking or executing the
model’s output rather than from judging it: mathematical tasks check against ground truth
using Math-Verify (Kydlíček, 2025), instruction-following prompts run formal constraint
checkers (Pyatkin et al., 2025), code runs against test cases in the sandbox, with a binary
reward for a perfect pass rate, and other tasks use exact match against gold answers. The
model emits final answers through a dedicated answer tool, which keeps the tool-calling
format of Section 4.2 in distribution during the stage. We evaluate a held-out slice of standard benchmarks continuously during training.
Two production configurations train in parallel, one with thinking disabled throughout
and one on a mixture of prompts with thinking enabled and disabled; the mixed configuration performs better and feeds the released models. Always-on thinking is not among
them: it produces overlong reasoning traces that slow training, and the model rarely calls

tools when thinking is enabled, which starves tool-use training of signal. We exclude tooluse prompts themselves from the production run after the hosted tool servers become a

RL starts from the selected long-context SFT checkpoints, after 4,200 long-context SFT steps
for the 8B model and 2,700 for the 70B model. The 8B RL run trains for 480 optimizer steps,
corresponding to roughly one million trajectories, and the 70B run for 180 steps. Figure 11
reports a few evaluation scores during the RL runs. We collect values with thinking mode
disabled, 4,096 maximum output tokens, four attempts per prompt, and the dedicated answer tool where it applies. Especially at the 8B scale, evaluation scores do not show a
plateau, hinting that longer training could have yielded further improvement.
Data. The prompt pool spans mathematics with verified answers (Big-Math-RLVerified (Albalak et al., 2025), DeepMath-103K (He et al., 2026)), instruction following with
checkable constraints from in-house prompt sets and from IFTrain (Pyatkin et al., 2025),
competitive programming (TACO (Li et al., 2023a), APPS (Hendrycks et al., 2021a), CodeContests (Li et al., 2022), Codeforces (Penedo et al., 2025)), procedurally generated reasoning tasks translated into 12 languages (Reasoning Gym (Stojanovski et al., 2025), translation
of traces using the framework introduced by Yukhymenko et al. (2026)), riddles (Lin et al.,
2021), tabular data manipulation tasks (Li et al., 2024c), legal examination multiple-choice
questions (LEXam (Fan et al., 2026)), and vision tasks, with thinking controlled per prompt.
Because a small subset of LEXam is part of the RL training mixture, we do not consider it
37

as a held-out evaluation benchmark. Table 10 reports the final data mixture distribution,
and we release its configuration with our verl fork.
4.6

A LIGNMENT

We deploy a two-stage approach to align the models with human preferences. First, we use
Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) (Rafailov et al., 2023) to align models with standard
qualitative aspects such as truthfulness, helpfulness, honesty, and instruction following.
Second, we use Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (SDPO) (Hübotter et al., 2026; Buening et al., 2026) to align the models with the Apertus charter (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026).
4.6.1

D IRECT P REFERENCE O PTIMIZATION

In the first step, we apply DPO to align the models with standard qualitative aspects such
as truthfulness, helpfulness, honesty, and instruction following. In particular, we leverage
both the standard algorithm and its online variant (Guo et al., 2024). While the former
expects a static dataset as input, which we describe below, the latter generates completions
for any new batch of prompts from the current version of the policy, calls a judge to select
the qualitatively good and bad responses, and updates the weights accordingly. In what
follows, we refer to the original DPO algorithm proposed by Rafailov et al. (2023) as offline
DPO to distinguish the two approaches. Our training recipe differs for Apertus 1.5 8B and
Apertus 1.5 70B. We align Apertus 1.5 8B with online DPO alone. However, we observe that
this approach is not immediately applicable to Apertus 1.5 70B, as it leads to qualitatively
short and brittle responses. Therefore, we include standard DPO training on a static dataset
before training with online DPO, which manages to improve the model’s capability across
the board. Section J.1 in Appendix J details the hyperparameter sweeps and ablations
driving these decisions.
Preference Data. Both online and offline DPO draw from the same prompt set, a filtered
and decontaminated subset of Dolci-Instruct-DPO (Team Olmo et al., 2025), whose sources
span reasoning, code, instruction following, multilingual chat, mathematics, and safety.
We keep the prompts and discard the original responses and preference labels. Decontamination against our evaluation suite and a 3,600-token length filter leave 245,898 preference
pairs. For the offline data we generate responses with a pool of 24 models spanning 0.5B
to 675B parameters, so that the pool spans a wide range of response quality. We then score
these prompt-completion pairs with Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 (Yang et al., 2025a),
using the rubric-based prompts of ActiveUltraFeedback (Melikidze et al., 2026). We elicit
scores on a one-to-five scale for truthfulness, helpfulness, honesty, and instruction following, taking the expected value under the judge’s distribution over the five rating tokens.
We take the final score as the average over the four aspects. We build each preference pair
by MaxMin selection, taking the highest-scoring response as chosen and the lowest-scoring
as rejected. Melikidze et al. (2026) compare this rule against a variety of response-pairing
strategies and find MaxMin the strongest of them, which is consistent with the Delta Learning Hypothesis, under which the gap between the two sides of a pair, rather than the absolute quality of either side, drives the gain (Geng et al., 2025). Neither stage trains on
multimodal or long-context data, and both run with thinking disabled. Appendix I gives
the rubric prompts and reports the per-source composition of the prompt set, the model
pool, and the score distribution of the resulting pairs.
Offline DPO. We begin preference alignment with offline DPO (Rafailov et al., 2023) for
Apertus 1.5 70B starting from its reinforcement-learning checkpoint (Section 4.5) and using
the dataset described above. Our implementation uses a length-normalized version (Meng
et al., 2024; Lambert et al., 2025) with KL regularization coefficient β = 25, learning rate
of 1 × 10−6 , linear learning rate scheduler with a warmup ratio of 0.1, the AdamW optimizer (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2019) (β 1 = 0.9, β 2 = 0.999, ϵ = 10−8 ) with no weight decay,
global batch size of 128, for one epoch at a maximum sequence length of 4,096 tokens.
38

Online DPO. We apply online DPO to both Apertus 1.5 8B and Apertus 1.5 70B, starting from the reinforcement-learning (Section 4.5) and the final offline DPO checkpoints,
respectively. This training uses the prompts from the Dolci-Instruct-DPO dataset while
discarding the offline responses. For each batch of prompts we sample rollouts from the
current policy with SGLang (Zheng et al., 2024) at temperature 1.0 and unrestricted top-p
and top-k, score them, and form one MaxMin pair per prompt. We then use this batch
of prompt-completion pairs to update the weights of the models with the DPO loss. We
sample 12 rollouts per prompt for Apertus 1.5 8B and eight for Apertus 1.5 70B, where the
smaller count is a concession to the cost of generation at that scale. To speed up training
and reduce the scoring latency, we deploy Qwen3.6-27B (Qwen Team, 2026a) as a judge
and use only the helpfulness aspect instead of the average of four. Section J.2 ablates both
departures: the four aspect scores are highly correlated, and Qwen3.6-27B matches or exceeds the offline stage’s much larger judge on external preference benchmarks despite the
size gap.
For both model sizes, online DPO uses KL penalty coefficient β = 0.1, a linear schedule
with a warmup ratio of 0.1, the AdamW optimizer (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2019) (β 1 = 0.9,
β 2 = 0.999, ϵ = 10−8 ) with a weight decay of 0.01, and prompt and response lengths of
2,048 tokens each. The prompt cap removes a further 3,210 prompts, so an online epoch
covers 242,688 of them. We train for one epoch on our verl fork with FSDP (Zhao et al.,
2023) and SGLang rollouts. Apertus 1.5 8B trains at a learning rate of 5 × 10−6 with
256 prompts per batch for 948 steps on 24 nodes, 16 for training and eight for judge annotation. Apertus 1.5 70B trains at a learning rate of 2.5 × 10−6 with 512 prompts per batch
for 474 steps on 144 nodes, 128 for training and 16 for annotation. Both runs complete in
under 12 hours. Table G.2 lists the full configurations.
Results. Preference alignment raises the overall average of our development benchmark
suite from 49.3% to 54.8% for Apertus 1.5 8B and from 55.3% to 61.2% for Apertus 1.5 70B.
The gains are broad. Knowledge rises by 13.0 and 2.3 points at the two scales, mathematics
by 4.1 and 4.8, reasoning by 2.6 and 2.7, and safety by 3.9 and 5.4, while cultural knowledge
rises by 8.7 points for Apertus 1.5 70B. Chat improves by 8.7 and 25.9 points, tool use by
13.1 and 12.5, and charter alignment by 22.5 and 21.3. Degenerate outputs become rarer at
both scales, falling from 1.7% to 0.6% and from 10.6% to 3.3%. The gains carry to thinking
mode although no stage trains with thinking enabled: the thinking-mode average rises by
4.2 points for Apertus 1.5 8B and 6.6 for Apertus 1.5 70B. Table 26, Table 27, Table 28, and
Section L.1 provide detailed evaluation results, discussed in Section 5.3.2.
4.6.2

C HARTER A LIGNMENT WITH SDPO

The final post-training stage aligns the model with the Apertus charter (Hernández-Cano
et al., 2026) through text-only self-distillation policy optimization (SDPO) (Hübotter et al.,
2026; Buening et al., 2026). During training, the student samples a response to a prompt.
A judge model receives that response together with the Apertus charter and writes textual
feedback on how the response can be improved to follow the charter more closely. We
form the teacher signal by calculating the log-probabilities of the initial student response
with the textual feedback added to the original user prompt. Conditioning on the feedback
in context shifts these log-probabilities toward a more charter-aligned version of the same
response. We then distill the teacher log-probabilities back into the student, which sees the
original prompt alone, with a top-k reverse KL loss. The stage is deliberately short and
lightweight, so that the capabilities built by the preceding stages carry over intact.
The two releases differ at this stage: Apertus 1.5 8B includes SDPO, whereas Apertus 1.5 70B ships the preference-aligned DPO checkpoint of Section 4.6. In ablations, initially SDPO regressed the capabilities of the larger model, most visibly on math capabilities, and we dropped the stage from the release for that reason. Investigating after the
release, we traced the regression not to SDPO itself but to an inconsistent tokenizer configuration between the DPO and the SDPO runs. Training is stable once the configuration is
fixed, though the gains remain modest next to those observed at 8B scale, increasing char39

ter alignment by 2.4% while most other capabilities are unchanged, the exceptions being a
1.1% gain on coding and a drop of 1.2% on chat capabilities.
Data. We train on value-laden safety prompts, where the charter shapes the response
most. The prompts come from the gretelai/gretel-safety-alignment-en-v1
dataset, of which we keep the prompts alone and discard the pre-generated responses. Decontamination against our evaluation suite leaves 8,354 prompts. We compare this against
Dolci-Instruct-DPO (Team Olmo et al., 2025), RealToxicityPrompts (Gehman et al., 2020),
and PRISM (Kirk et al., 2024), and find that the Gretel safety prompts align the model best.
Growing the dataset beyond this size does not have a measurable advantage, so we train
on it alone to keep the stage cheap.
Feedback. The judge is Qwen3.6-27B (Qwen Team, 2026a), chosen over self-reflection, in
which the student itself, the DPO checkpoint, critiques its own responses. Both variants
train comparably and self-reflection aligns better with the charter, reaching a win rate of
73.2% in comparison to 67.4%, but it costs roughly 3% on math, so we adopt the Qwen
judge. We further tune the feedback and teacher templates by hand over successive runs,
guided by benchmark performance and by the rollouts of the preceding run. Appendix G
gives both prompt templates. The remaining hyperparameters matter little at this scale,
since the small learning rate and the short schedule limit how far the model can change.
Training. We train in verl with vLLM (Kwon et al., 2023) rollouts and thinking disabled
throughout. Starting from the DPO checkpoint, we run 117 steps at a batch size of 64,
sampling 16 rollouts per prompt, at a learning rate of 1 × 10−7 with 10 warmup steps, and
distill the top-64 tokens plus one tail bucket that absorbs the remainder of the distribution.
The run takes two hours on 32 nodes of four GH200 GPUs each.
4.7

SFT A BLATIONS

Multimodal SFT corpora usually run to tens of millions of conversations, an order of magnitude above our text mixture of 3.7M, and most of them carry reasoning traces (Wen et al.,
2026; Cai et al., 2026; Li et al., 2025a; NVIDIA et al., 2026). Introducing them changes two
things at once: text becomes a minority of the mixture, and a reasoning trace, a synthetic
derivation written by a larger model that precedes the visible answer and that our chat
template places in the thinking span (Section 4.1), enters about a third of the training conversations.
This motivates four questions:
Q1. How does adding multimodal SFT data change the text capabilities relative to textonly SFT?
Q2. What does supervising reasoning traces add, on text and on multimodal tasks?
Q3. Do the gains from trace supervision persist when reasoning is disabled at inference
time, or do they require the model to reason?
Q4. How do off-policy reasoning traces from stronger models during SFT shape what
subsequent RL can achieve?
Q1–Q3. We build the SFT ablations from the cleaned text mixture and a selected subset of
the multimodal SFT pool, after decontamination against the evaluation benchmarks, exact
deduplication, and answer-format normalization. We construct paired versions of each
eligible example with and without reasoning traces, referred to as the CoT and No-CoT
arms. Within each pair, the prompt, any multimodal inputs, and the visible answer are
identical; only the reasoning trace differs. Traces are retained from the source data where
available and generated with Qwen3.8-27B for selected vision examples; a trace is kept
only when its conclusion agrees with the visible answer.
Table 11 summarizes the four training configurations. Text No-CoT versus MM No-CoT
isolates the effect of adding multimodal data, while MM No-CoT versus MM CoT isolates
40

reasoning supervision. Comparing Text CoT with MM CoT further measures the contribution of multimodal reasoning traces beyond text reasoning alone. All configurations initialize from the final Apertus 1.5 8B long-context pretraining checkpoint (Section 3.3) with
a fresh optimizer state and are trained for two epochs. We use a sequence length of 8,192
with best-fit packing, a global batch size of 1,024 sequences (8.4M tokens per update), and
a constant learning rate of 8 × 10−5 after 28 warmup updates. The multimodal configurations are trained with three seeds and the text-only configurations with one. We evaluate
every configuration with thinking forced off or on, banning the opening token or prefilling
it, since after SFT enabling thinking alone does not reliably make the model reason.

tokens over the full two-epoch run. CoT and No-CoT arms use the same conversations
and differ only in whether reasoning traces are retained.

Figure 12: SFT ablations of multimodal supervision and reasoning, one panel per question.
Q1 compares text benchmark scores for MM No-CoT versus Text No-CoT and MM CoT
versus Text CoT, evaluated under matched response modes. Q2 compares MM CoT with
thinking on against MM No-CoT with thinking off. Q3 compares MM CoT against MM
No-CoT, both with thinking off. Bars report mean score differences in percentage points;
dots denote the three multimodal seeds. Comparisons use a single text-only reference seed
in Q1 and paired seeds in Q2 and Q3. Scores are unweighted means over 16 text benchmarks, excluding MGSM (Table H.1 gives the reason), and 14 vision benchmarks. Without
thinking, decoding is greedy with a 16,384-token budget; thinking is sampled at temperature 0.6 and top-p 0.95 with a 32,768-token budget.
Figure 12 answers Q1 to Q3 with one panel each. For Q1, adding multimodal SFT data
preserves text performance; when multimodal reasoning traces are retained, text performance even improves on average over the model trained only on text reasoning. For Q2,
reasoning-trace supervision provides a clear benefit on multimodal tasks when reasoning
is enabled at inference time, while its effect on aggregate text performance remains negligible. However, for Q3, the multimodal benefit turns into a small loss when reasoning
is disabled: trace supervision lowers vision scores by 2.2 points on average, consistently
across all three seeds, while text is unaffected. The averages hide large per-benchmark
effects (Section H.1 in Appendix H): with thinking on, trace supervision adds seven to
41

18 points on mathematics but costs 12 to 14 on IFEval and Multi-IF, mostly because about
a quarter of traces degenerate into repetition and never finish.
Takeaway. Multimodal supervision can be integrated at scale during SFT without sacrificing text capabilities, and reasoning-trace supervision pays off primarily when the model
is allowed to reason at inference time.
Q4. Whether reasoning supervision during SFT provides a better starting point for subsequent RL remains unsettled. In text reasoning, prior work finds both that SFT can constrain later exploration (Kang et al., 2026) and that stronger reasoning SFT can improve
the initialization for RL (Liu et al., 2026); off-policy reasoning traces can also be beneficial
when combined with on-policy exploration (Yan et al., 2025). Evidence from multimodal
reasoning is similarly mixed (Chen et al., 2025a; Wei et al., 2025), but comes largely from
encoder-based VLMs.
We test the same question in our unified discrete-token model. Here, the shift from SFT to
RL introduces two distinct mismatches. First, the SFT reasoning traces are generated offpolicy by stronger teacher models, whereas RL uses rollouts from the student itself. Second, much of the SFT reasoning supervision is conditioned on multimodal inputs, whereas
the RL of this ablation is entirely text-only (Table 12). Thus, the multimodal reasoning trajectories learned during SFT are never directly revisited during RL.
We make the controlled comparison between Text CoT and the seed-42 MM CoT checkpoint from the preceding SFT ablation. We additionally include the production SFT checkpoint of Section 4.2 as a retrospective reference. Unlike the two ablation checkpoints, it
is trained on a broader, more heterogeneous mixture without the same pairing and normalization controls, and is therefore not a controlled arm. From each checkpoint, we run
RL using our fork of NeMo RL (NVIDIA, 2025)7 separately on mathematics, reasoning, and
instruction-following prompts (Table 12). Instruction following is trained with thinking off,
since our SFT mixture supervises it almost entirely without reasoning traces and explicit
reasoning can hurt instruction following (Li et al., 2025b; Fu et al., 2026). Figure 13 tracks
held-out performance, while Figure 14 shows the corresponding reward and responselength dynamics.
Table 12: Training configuration of the RL ablation, with one row per prompt set. The
sets are drawn from DAPO-Math-17k (Yu et al., 2025), Reasoning Gym (Stojanovski et al.,
2025), and nvidia/Nemotron-RL-instruction_following. Each run lasts 200 steps
of GRPO (Shao et al., 2024), with 64 prompts and eight rollouts per step. We use binary rewards, a leave-one-out baseline, DAPO asymmetric clipping, no KL penalty, and a constant
learning rate of 2 × 10−6 . Rollouts are asynchronous, at most two policy versions stale, and
capped at 14,336 new tokens with thinking on and 3,840 with thinking off. Mathematics
is scored with Math-Verify (Kydlíček, 2025); for reasoning tasks, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B is used
as a fallback judge only when the task verifier rejects an answer.

Across mathematics and reasoning, MM CoT retains or increases its advantage over Text
CoT throughout RL, despite the latter substantially increasing its reasoning length; on instruction following, where SFT contains little trace supervision, the two converge. These
results suggest that the gains introduced by multimodal reasoning supervision are not
washed out by subsequent text-only RL, but can persist through on-policy optimization.

5

E VALUATIONS

This chapter evaluates the Apertus 1.5 models. Section 5.1 fixes the shared methodology:
the harnesses, the benchmark suites, the judge models, and the pitfalls we control for. We
then follow the models through the pipeline: the base models after continued pretraining,
the post-trained models on general text capabilities including thinking mode, and their
multilingual and translation behavior. The new modalities follow, image and audio understanding, and then the remaining capability areas: long context, tool use, and security and
safety. Qualitative spot-testing closes the chapter with the behavior that benchmarks miss.
5.1

E VALUATION S ETUP

We evaluate text capabilities with our fork of the LM Evaluation Harness (Gao et al., 2024),
driven by a launcher layer that fixes task lists, prompt formats, and the serving configuration, so that runs are comparable across checkpoints. Visual and audio benchmarks
run on forks of LMMs-Eval (Zhang et al., 2025) and VLMEvalKit (Duan et al., 2024) behind a common dispatcher. The forks and the task configurations we report are public.
The text suite starts from the 27 benchmarks reported for Apertus 1.0 and grows to a release suite of roughly 50 tasks spanning general knowledge, instruction following and chat
quality, mathematics, coding, reasoning, multilingual and cultural knowledge, bias, toxicity and safety, system-prompt guardrails, function calling, and alignment with the Apertus
charter. Additions for this release include machine translation, hallucination, over-refusal,
function calling, multi-turn instruction following, and the charter-alignment benchmark.
Many knowledge and mathematics benchmarks use chain-of-thought variants scored on
extracted answers, matching contemporary open-model practice. Before a benchmark enters the suite, we validate the implementation by reproducing published scores of openly
released models; we exclude from the reported set tasks whose published scores we cannot
reproduce, or whose answer extraction proves unreliable.
Benchmarks that require a judge use openly released models served on Alps: a Llama3.3-70B-Instruct judge (e.g., for AlpacaEval), Llama-2-13B-cls for HarmBench (Mazeika et al.,
2024), and a Llama-Guard-4 safety classifier for RealToxicityPrompts (Gehman et al., 2020).
Where the original benchmark prescribes a different judge, absolute scores are not directly
comparable with the original leaderboard, and the affected sections say so.
We evaluate post-trained models through the chat template. Thinking mode is deactivated
by default, and we report it separately. During development we additionally monitor a
held-out set of benchmarks outside the training distribution (Table 27) to detect overfitting
of the post-training recipe to the development suite.
Known Pitfalls. Several classes of error distort benchmark numbers, and we control for
them explicitly. Harness defaults are tuned for base models: few-shot prompting, greedy
decoding, and tight output-token budgets under-report post-trained models, and especially thinking models, whose answers are truncated or missed by answer extraction.
Small benchmarks need repeated sampled runs before differences are meaningful. Tooluse scores depend on the parser matching the call format the model emits. Serving-side effects, such as prompt-formatting differences and checkpoint-conversion errors, shift scores
silently; the two serving backends are not numerically identical, so no reported series mixes
them.
The full list of benchmarks, including relevant settings for replication, is given in Appendix L.
5.2

B ASE M ODELS

Table 13 to Table 22 compare the Apertus 1.5 base checkpoints against their Apertus 1.0 predecessors on the identical suite, with open base models as context: the difference quantifies
what continued pretraining adds in text capability while introducing the new modalities.
Section 3.4 discusses how these capabilities evolve over the course of training.
44

The evaluations show that the final Apertus 1.5 base model improves on a wide range of
tasks compared to Apertus 1.0. Apertus 1.5 8B specifically compares well with fully open
peer models. In categories such as general language understanding and factual knowledge, Apertus base models have mostly closed the gap to other open-source models. Previously a weak point, performance for reasoning, math, and coding has strongly improved
compared to the 1.0 model releases. Apertus models remain strong in multilingual tasks,
despite small regressions.

5.3

P OST-T RAINED M ODELS

Table 23 and Table 24 report the release evaluation runs of the final checkpoints against
Apertus 1.0 and contemporary peers, all with thinking disabled; the thinking-mode comparison follows in Table 25. Each table splits the peers into fully open models, which release
weights, data, and training recipes, and open-weight models. We evaluate our 8B model
against other fully open models: OLMo 3 7B Instruct (Team Olmo et al., 2025), Marin 8B
Instruct (The Marin Project, 2025), and EuroLLM 9B Instruct-2512 (Ramos et al., 2026). We
also report scores for the open-weight models Qwen3.5-9B (Qwen Team, 2026b) and Llama
3.1 8B Instruct (Grattafiori et al., 2024).
We report results for Apertus 1.5 70B along with other open models above 22B parameters
in size. We compare to fully open models OLMo 3.1 32B Instruct (Team Olmo et al., 2025) by
AI2, K2-V2 Instruct (K2 Team et al., 2025), and EuroLLM (Ramos et al., 2026). Additionally,
we report results for the open-weight models Llama 3.3 70B Instruct (Grattafiori et al., 2024),
Gemma 4 31B (Gemma Team et al., 2026), Qwen 3.5 27B (Qwen Team, 2026b), and Nemotron
3.5 Lightning 30B-A3B (NVIDIA et al., 2025).
The generational step is clear: Apertus 1.5 8B improves over Apertus 1.0 8B Instruct on
every reported benchmark except TruthfulQA and GPQA (Table 23). Among the fully
open models of its class, the 8B leads on knowledge, instruction following, and cultural
benchmarks, while OLMo 3 leads on mathematics and code; across all compared models
of the class, including open-weight ones, it is the strongest on instruction following (IFEval,
Multi-IF, and IFBench) and AGIEval. We report separate scores for the subsequent steps of
post-training in the following Section 5.3.2.
Apertus 1.5 70B improves over its predecessor on every benchmark and outperforms
EuroLLM-22B on nearly all benchmarks, but trails the strongest open-weight contemporaries on most axes (Table 24).
We omit the judged Arena-Hard scores from this snapshot, as the judge model is unavailable in these runs; we report AIME with the thinking comparison in Table 25.
5.3.1

R EASONING AND T HINKING M ODE

Thinking is a request-time flag of the chat template rather than a separate model, and we
evaluate every checkpoint with thinking on and off, at a 32,768-token thinking budget. For
small benchmarks with a high run-to-run variance, like AIME, we report mean@k metrics
across multiple seeds (e.g., with k = 8 for AIME). Table 25 compares the Apertus models
in both modes with thinking-native peers in their thinking mode. We compare to OLMo
3 7B Think (Team Olmo et al., 2025), OLMo 3.1 32B Think (Team Olmo et al., 2025), and K2
Think V2 (K2 Think Team et al., 2026). The improvement concentrates where reasoning
is long: on the 8B, MATH-500 improves from 51.2 to 71.4, MATH from 41.2 to 57.3, and
IFBench from 35.3 to 44.3, the strongest IFBench score among the compared 8B-class models; multimodal reasoning gains double digits, roughly 10 points on MMMU. Knowledge
benchmarks move less, and the 70B even cedes GPQA under thinking. For AIME 24/25,
while still behind other peer models, Apertus has improved dramatically since version 1.0,
when the models were unable to solve a substantial number of tasks. Thinking further
pushes Apertus 1.5 8B from 7.5 to 22.9 on AIME 24, and from 8.3 to 23.8 on AIME 25.
5.3.2

P OST-T RAINING L INEAGE

Our post-training pipeline runs in four stages: supervised finetuning, including its longcontext stage (Section 4.2 and Section 4.3), reinforcement learning (Section 4.5), preference alignment with DPO (Section 4.6), and charter alignment with SDPO (Section 4.6.2).
Table 26 follows both models through that sequence, each column adding one stage to
the checkpoint of the column before it, so the difference between adjacent columns isolates what the intervening stage contributes. We split the suite in two: the development benchmarks, which guide our own iteration, and the held-out benchmarks of Table 27, which we never consult when selecting checkpoints and which therefore measure
how far the gains generalize. Table 28 repeats the exercise with thinking enabled, and

Section L.1 gives all 37 development benchmarks. In these tables, including Table L.3,
GPT-4.1 (gpt-4.1-2025-04-14) judges AlpacaEval and Arena-Hard v0.1, and o3-mini
(o3-mini-2025-01-31) judges Arena-Hard v2.
Every stage adds to the overall average, which rises from 47.3% to 55.0% for Apertus 1.5 8B
and from 52.2% to 61.2% for Apertus 1.5 70B, and the held-out averages move with them,
from 48.2% to 51.7% and from 53.2% to 56.4%. The stages divide the labor. Reinforcement
learning does most of the work on reasoning and instruction following, lifting ACPBench
by 5.1 and 11.4 points at the two scales and IFEval by 5.6 and 7.3. Preference alignment
then takes the conversational and agentic axes: AlpacaEval gains 12.1 points at 8B and 33.5
at 70B, Arena-Hard v0.1 gains 13.9 and 38.8, and tool use gains 13.1 and 12.5. It also carries
most of the safety movement, with BBQ rising 12.4 points at 70B and ToxiGen 8.6 at 8B.
SDPO is deliberately narrow, moving charter alignment by 9.9 points and AlpacaEval by
10.0, and leaving the remaining benchmarks within a few points.
Two effects run against the trend. Reinforcement learning shortens responses at 70B scale,
from 367 to 253 tokens on average over the three chat benchmarks, and offline DPO raises
them to 495, the behavior that motivates the offline stage of Section 4.6. The alignment
stages also make both models markedly more cautious: refusals on benign prompts that
resemble harmful ones climb sharply through the alignment stages (Section 5.9.2).

M ULTILINGUAL AND C ULTURAL E VALUATION

Multilinguality is where Apertus stands strongest relative to its peers. The 13-point gap to
Qwen3.5-9B on English knowledge benchmarks (Table 23) shrinks to two or three points
on the multilingual and cultural slice (Table 29); Apertus 1.5 8B is the strongest of all compared models on multilingual ARC and Multi-IF and among the strongest on multilingual
TruthfulQA, and its lead over the fully open group is wide. Thinking transfers across
languages, lifting Global-MMLU by eight points. The clearest weakness is XNLI, which
regresses against Apertus 1.0 at both scales; Apertus 1.5 70B improves on its predecessor
everywhere else (Table 30) but trails the strongest open-weight contemporaries.
5.4.1

M ACHINE T RANSLATION

Being able to translate well is a major sign of a model’s multilingual capability, and machine translation remains a challenging NLP task (Zouhar et al., 2026a). To this end, we
evaluate several comparable models across many languages on WMT24++ (Kocmi et al.,
2024; Deutsch et al., 2025) and its Romansh extension (Vamvas et al., 2025), WMT25 (Kocmi
et al., 2025), and SwissGov (Wastl et al., 2026). This evaluation pays particular attention to,
but is not limited to, German, Swiss German, French, Italian, and Romansh.
Automatic Evaluation. The results of automatic evaluation in Table 32 and Table 31,
based on the ChrF (Popović, 2015) and COMETKiwi22 (Rei et al., 2022) metrics respectively, show that the average performance of Apertus models correlates across languages

5.4

with other models, indicating that they do not tend to have an inherent preference for
a particular language. An exception is translation from German into Romansh varieties,
where the targeted post-training (Section 4.2.3) enables Apertus 1.5 8B to surpass comparable models by a large margin.
Human Evaluation. Automatic evaluation can sometimes be misaligned with human
perception of quality (Lavie et al., 2025; Kocmi et al., 2025). To this end, we also run human evaluation of translation quality, where bilingual annotators (recruited from authors
and their professional network) are blindly presented with multiple translations and rate
them from 0% to 100%. We carry out the annotations using the cESA annotation protocol (Zouhar et al., 2026b; Zouhar & Kocmi, 2026) with four model outputs shown next to
each other: Apertus 1.0 8B, Apertus 1.5 8B, EuroLLM 22B, and Ministral 3 8B. In Table 33
the human evaluation confirms that Apertus 1.5 8B avoids critical failure on Switzerlandspecific language pairs. The data collected by human annotators are available publicly.8
Automatic Evaluation with SwissGov. SwissGov is a multiparallel document-level
dataset of Swiss government texts in three official national languages and English, providing a domain-specific evaluation that approximates a realistic use case for Apertus. For
language pairs also covered by WMT, evaluation with SwissGov yields the same relative
ranking, suggesting that the observed performance differences generalize beyond the news
domain.
Last Translation Benchmark. We also evaluate Apertus models on the hard translation
examples (Zouhar et al., 2026a) over more than 100 languages and 3000+ texts. Apertus v1.5 8B shows promising performance, achieving 1.32% on LTBv1-eval (911 samples),
matching the GPT-OSS-20B score and outperforming models like Claude Haiku 4.5 or
Google Translate. In comparison, the Apertus v1 8B model (Apertus-8B-Instruct-2509) and
the 70B model (Apertus-70B-Instruct-2509) achieve 0.77% and 1.21%, respectively, while
the v1.5 70B model shows a worse result with 0.66%. We believe that our bigger model is
better adapted to English-centric, high-performance tasks, while the 8B model is a fitting
candidate for translation. Moreover, the results give a strong signal toward more focus on
multilingual and multicultural post-training, which we plan to target in future versions
with greater effort.
5.5

I MAGE U NDERSTANDING

The image suite spans around 70 unique benchmarks, from general visual question answering and OCR to spatial reasoning, remote sensing, and medical imagery; Table 34
reports a representative subset for the released checkpoints against openly released visionlanguage models, evaluated with our forks of LMMs-Eval (Zhang et al., 2025) and
VLMEvalKit (Duan et al., 2024). Decoding is greedy with a 16,384-token budget; thinking
samples at temperature 0.6 and top-p 0.95 with a 32,768-token budget. Spatial-intelligence
benchmarks follow the EASI protocol (Cai et al., 2025). Within the fully open group, Apertus 1.5 8B leads on document and chart understanding (ChartQA, DocVQA) and general
VQA (GQA), while Molmo2-8B leads general perception. The strongest open-weight models, Qwen3-VL and Qwen2.5-VL, stay ahead overall, most visibly on OCR-heavy tasks.
Thinking lifts reasoning-heavy benchmarks by roughly 12 points (MMMU 39.8 to 52.2) but
costs some extraction-style tasks about as much (ChartQA 78.7 to 66.0). Apertus 1.5 70B
adds little over the 8B on most image benchmarks.

5.6

A UDIO U NDERSTANDING

The audio suite centers on multilingual speech recognition, the primary target of the audio
training, complemented by speech translation and by audio understanding beyond transcription, from audio and music question answering to vocal-sound classification. Table 35
reports a representative subset against openly released audio models, namely Qwen2.5Omni (Xu et al., 2025), Qwen2-Audio (Chu et al., 2024), and Kimi-Audio (Kimi Team et al.,
2025), all evaluated with our fork of LMMs-Eval (Zhang et al., 2025). It covers speech
recognition on LibriSpeech (Panayotov et al., 2015), VoxPopuli (Wang et al., 2021a), SPGISpeech (O’Neill et al., 2021), TED-LIUM (Hernandez et al., 2018), and FLEURS (Conneau
et al., 2023); speech translation on CoVoST2 (Wang et al., 2021b); and audio understanding
on MMAU (Sakshi et al., 2025), MuChoMusic (Weck et al., 2024), Clotho-AQA (Lipping
et al., 2022), and VocalSound (Gong et al., 2022).
We evaluate every model ourselves, on the same tasks with the same scoring and each
task’s instruction as the prompt, so that the values are comparable. Qwen2-Audio
keeps the default of its chat template, and Kimi-Audio is prompted as in its authors’
own evaluation (MoonshotAI/Kimi-Audio-Evalkit), with the instruction in the user
turn and no system prompt, which its inference code (MoonshotAI/Kimi-Audio) does
not accept. Qwen2.5-Omni receives the task-category prompts of its authors’ examples
(QwenLM/Qwen2.5-Omni) for speech recognition, speech translation, and vocal-sound
classification; for audio question answering, for which its authors publish no evaluation
prompt, it receives a short audio-understanding prompt, because with its default prompt
it appends conversation to one-word answers. We report MMAU on its test-mini split, the
split with public answers (Sakshi et al., 2025). One CoVoST2 clip cannot be decoded in the
pipeline of the three peers; we score their empty answers as wrong, so that every model is
scored on the same samples.
On English speech recognition of short utterances, the dedicated audio models lead,
by wide margins on LibriSpeech and SPGISpeech, while Apertus 1.5 70B comes within
2.1 points of the best VoxPopuli error rate and ahead of Qwen2.5-Omni and Qwen2-Audio.
On FLEURS, Apertus 1.5 70B holds the best word error rates on Spanish and Ukrainian,
and Apertus 1.5 8B the best on Polish, where Qwen2.5-Omni reaches 87.1%, Qwen2-Audio
98.6%, and Kimi-Audio 65.3%; the peers lead English, German, French, and Italian. On
TED-LIUM long form, whose eight talks run 3 to 22 minutes, Apertus 1.5 8B reaches 36.0%
and Apertus 1.5 70B 47.3%, against 61.8% for Kimi-Audio, 67.7% for Qwen2.5-Omni, and
99.6% for Qwen2-Audio, which processes audio in 30-second windows and returns at most
26 words per talk. On CoVoST2 English-Chinese, the Qwen models lead with 44.3 BLEU
for Qwen2.5-Omni and 38.1 for Qwen2-Audio, against 15.3 for Apertus 1.5 8B, 13.1 for
Apertus 1.5 70B, and 0.3 for Kimi-Audio. On audio understanding beyond speech, Apertus trails the peers throughout, and its vocal-sound classification is at or below chance
(16.0% and 5.0% against 16.7% for six classes).

Table 35: Audio evaluation of the released Apertus 8B and 70B checkpoints against open
audio models. Word error rates (WER) are in percent, lower is better; accuracies are in
percent and BLEU on a 0 to 100 scale, both higher is better. Best per row in bold. All models
decode greedily, Qwen2-Audio with the repetition penalty (Keskar et al., 2019) of 1.1 from
its generation configuration, which its authors’ evaluation code (QwenLM/Qwen2-Audio)
also applies, and use each task’s output cap; where a task sets none, all models share a cap
of 4,096 tokens, which TED-LIUM long form also receives because its own cap of 256 tokens
would cut off transcripts of up to 4,164 words; on that task, Qwen2.5-Omni uses PyTorch
SDPA attention, whose fused kernels do not store the full attention matrix (Dao et al., 2022;
Rabe & Staats, 2021), because with the default eager attention its longest talks exceed GPU
memory.

5.7

L ONG C ONTEXT

We evaluate long-context ability with two benchmarks: RULER (Hsieh et al., 2024) measures synthetic retrieval and aggregation, while HELMET (Yen et al., 2025) spans seven
categories of downstream tasks.9 Table 36 reports both at every stage of the Apertus 1.5 8B
and 70B pipelines. The context extension is the decisive step: on RULER it puts Apertus
1.5 ahead of Apertus 1.0 at every length and at both sizes, and it carries the model out to
256k tokens. Short-context SFT then undoes much of that, collapsing retrieval on long inputs, and the dedicated long-context SFT stage described in Section 4.3 restores it. RL and
preference optimization add further gains on RULER even though neither stage trains on
long documents, a point we return to in Section 3.4. HELMET, in the right-hand columns,
shows the same collapse and the same recovery, with the preference-optimization stages
strongest at every length. Through 128k the 70B pipeline stays ahead of the 8B on both
benchmarks.

T OOL U SE

We evaluate function calling on the single-turn categories of BFCL v3, with the parser
matched to the call format of the chat template (Table 37); the official leaderboard additionally includes multi-turn categories, so these numbers are not comparable to published
entries. Both models improve over their predecessors, the 70B most visibly: its overall accuracy rises from 58.0 to 70.1, within 1.5 points of Llama-3.3-70B, and it repairs the parallelcall collapse of Apertus 1.0. The 8B remains weak on parallel calls, a known release limitation, but nearly doubles its predecessor’s score on the irrelevance categories, where the
declared tools do not fit the request and the model should answer in plain text instead
of calling one. This is the intended effect of a supervised-finetuning design choice: tool
declarations are also injected into conversations that call no tool (Section 4.2). At launch,
providers expose tool calling and thinking as mutually exclusive, a serving limitation discussed with the chat template.

S ECURITY, S AFETY, AND I NTERPRETABILITY

As Apertus is a fully open and massively multilingual foundation model, the safety evaluations of the Apertus family present a number of unique challenges, initially outlined
for the Apertus 1.0 release (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026). While most current LLM literature, standards, and regulations focus on LLM deployments, Apertus is an open foundation
LLM, for which release risks have to be evaluated accordingly. First, any security mechanisms relying on alignment, such as refusal, can be removed by post-release training, so
the intrinsic model usefulness for dangerous capabilities must be evaluated independently
of alignment (O’Brien et al., 2026). Second, pre-release evaluations of models’ safety are
inherently distinct from performance evaluation. While benchmarks can serve as good
general proxies of performance, security evaluations seek to evaluate the severity and likelihood of potential harms arising from the model release, with a single high-likelihood,
high-impact risk being able to block the model release.
To ensure the released model’s safety in light of those considerations, we approach model
safety in three stages.
First, we design the post-training to ensure safety in non-adversarial usage scenarios. The
SFT mixture carries only about 16,000 refusal-style safety samples out of 3.7 million (Section 4.2), so safety behavior is concentrated in the alignment stages, most directly in the
charter alignment of SDPO. Over-refusal is tracked as the counterweight throughout development. Of the safety suite, only BBQ, ToxiGen, and WMDP inform alignment iteration;
every other safety benchmark is held out as an out-of-distribution check.
Second, we evaluate release-candidate models against standardized safety and security
benchmarks. Notably, we use HarmBench Direct Requests and Human Jailbreaks (referred
to as HarmBench**) as a proxy for the safety of the release candidates in non-adversarial
use, thanks to good coverage of general harm categories (Mazeika et al., 2024). We use
WMDP as a proxy for intrinsically dangerous knowledge, but without seeking to optimize it, due to overlap with general model capabilities (Li et al., 2024b). Finally, we use
BBQ, ToxiGen, and RealToxicityPrompts*10 to measure the potential for unprompted and
unexpected toxicity in English, and PolyGloToxicityPrompts to validate it in other languages (Parrish et al., 2022; Hartvigsen et al., 2022; Gehman et al., 2020; Jain et al., 2024).
10 For scalability, we randomly subsample RealToxicityPrompts and use the LLaMA-Guard3 judge
model, as reported in the Apertus 1.0 technical report.

60

Finally, we perform a blocking manual evaluation of every release-candidate model and
provide early access to independent third parties. As part of the manual evaluation, we
notably evaluate CBRNE, dual-use, and child sexual abuse usefulness, ensuring a high
coverage of risk criteria outlined in NIST-RMF GenAI 600.1 (Autio et al., 2024) and ML
Commons Risk 1.0 (Ghosh et al., 2025) (cf. Table N.1 in Appendix N for detailed mapping).
Consistent with the concern outlined above for open-weight model release, to ensure that
we evaluate the intrinsic capabilities of the models rather than alignment, we create and
evaluate abliterated versions of each release-candidate model (Arditi et al., 2024) and provide access to them to external evaluators. Due to the nature of evaluation of usefulness for
inherently malicious use, the manual evaluation criteria per se can be useful to malicious
parties and hence are not publicly released. However, more detailed information about
the manual evaluation and third parties is available in Appendix M, and the pre-release
manual review report is available upon request.
5.9.1

S AFETY B ENCHMARK P ERFORMANCE

We report safety benchmark performance in Table 38 for the released checkpoints, their
predecessors, and open-weight anchors, using the served classifier and judge models of
Section 5.1. The alignment stages move the metrics in the intended direction for Apertus
1.5 vs 1.0: ToxiGen rises by 11.4% for the 8B and 24.3% for the 70B, BBQ improves at both
sizes with the 70B reaching the best score in the comparison, and the judged toxicity of
generated continuations is low throughout. WMDP rises slightly alongside general capability (2.1% and 1.8% for 8B and 70B respectively). On HarmBench, the released 8B resists
direct harmful requests (1.3% attack success) but remains vulnerable to human-written
jailbreaks (31.5%), for an average request success rate of 26.5%. This is consistent with
the main defense against malicious misuse of the Apertus family being capability limitations, rather than alignment, and is still an improvement of 8.7% and 2.8% for the 8B and
70B respectively. Finally, we evaluate Apertus 1.5 release candidates for multimodal jailbreak robustness, leveraging MM-SafetyBench (Liu et al., 2025a), obtaining scores of 38.0%
and 28.5% for the 8B and 70B respectively (lower is better). Comparatively, Qwen3-VL8B, Molmo2-8B, Gemma4-12B, and EuroVLM-9B achieve 22.0%, 25.1%, 18.0%, and 52.1%,
respectively, positioning the released models within the normal distribution of similarly
sized open-weight models (full data in Table 34; note that the values are inverted in the
table to facilitate comparison).

5.9.2

O VER -R EFUSAL AND H ALLUCINATION

Two additions to the evaluation suite measure failure modes adjacent to safety. We measure
over-refusal, the refusal of benign requests, on the hard subset of OR-Bench (Cui et al.,
2025a) as a counterweight to the refusal-based safety benchmarks. Refusal rates rise from
3.6% after SFT to 81.5% after alignment at 8B and from 2.9% to 67.6% at 70B (Table L.3).
61

Most of the rise comes at the DPO stage; at 70B, RL already raises the rate to 24.7%. A share
of the benchmark’s prompts is legitimately refusable, so we report this trend rather than
an absolute over-refusal rate (Section 7.2). We measure hallucination with HalluLens (Bang
et al., 2025). Our judge is a locally served Qwen3.5-27B, whereas the original benchmark
uses a Llama-3.1-405B, so absolute scores are not comparable with the official leaderboard;
a cross-check with a Llama-3.3-70B judge moves the rates by at most two points. Table 39
shows a split result. Where the failure is answering about things that do not exist, Apertus
1.5 improves sharply: abstention on fabricated entities more than triples at 8B, and longform generation becomes both more precise and more willing to abstain, gaining six points
of F1 at 8B and two at 70B. Short-form hallucination on questions about real entities does
not improve. Both releases attempt an answer on more than eight of 10 questions and
hallucinate on roughly six to seven of them, slightly more than Apertus 1.0 at 70B scale.
Reducing this without inflating over-refusal remains open for a future release.

9.4

I NTERPRETABILITY

We do not claim to understand the internal behavior of the released models, and the results
above make the gap explicit: the preceding benchmarks tell us about the frequency of
failures, not why they arise. To be open about potential risks, we treat interpretability as
one safeguard in a “defense-in-depth” strategy (Bengio et al., 2026), alongside evaluation,
alignment, and manual safety review.
With this release, we launch Apertus Claritas11 , a public platform that collects interpretability studies on the Apertus models, including exploratory and negative results. Importantly,
open weights and intermediate checkpoints let us study when an unsafe behavior forms (Biderman et al., 2026), rather than only after training. The studies to date use the Apertus 1.0
and 1.5 checkpoints; three of their findings feed into this release.
Exploding Activations. Hallucination probes trained on the activations of the 8B initially fail where the same probes succeed on Llama-3.1-8B (Obeso et al., 2025). The
cause is activation norms that grow roughly a hundredfold toward the deeper layers. With layer normalization and precision fixes, the probe reaches an AUC of 0.90
(swiss-ai/feature-probes). The diagnosis motivates revisiting how the activationfunction parameters are regularized in continued pretraining (Section 3.2).
11 https://apertus-claritas.org

62

Degeneration Probes. Linear probes on the hidden states of the 8B detect repetitive
collapse before it surfaces in the generated text, most reliably from layer 15 onward
(swiss-ai/degeneration-probe). This points toward intervening during generation
rather than diagnosing afterward, and complements the degeneration rates tracked across
the post-training stages (Section 5.3.2).
Multilingual Geometry. Ablations on the translation directions of the 8B show
that low-resource languages lean on the representations of related high-resource
languages: for example, suppressing the high-resource direction degrades Galician and Afrikaans translation, while the reverse intervention has almost no effect
(swiss-ai/multilingual-geometry). This gives a mechanism behind the coverage
picture of Section 5.4 and suggests that long-tail quality needs more than additional data.
Further platform studies cover error-predicting probes with calibrated inference-time
steering (swiss-ai/apertus-probes) (Hedström et al., 2025), emotion persona representations (van der Ben et al., 2026), and bias auditing of latent generation paths (Pelossi
et al., 2026).
5.10

Q UALITATIVE S POT-T ESTING

Hand testing during development repeatedly surfaces behavior that the benchmark suite
misses, including degeneration on out-of-distribution prompts and weak multi-turn instruction following; this section documents that practice and its findings. User reports on
Apertus 1.0 concentrated on a failure mode the benchmarks had not measured: degeneration in long conversations, particularly under language switching. Hand testing of Apertus 1.5 adds identity confusion, where the model misattributes itself to another provider in
some languages (Section 7.2). The SFT mixture references the Apertus identity (Section 4.2)
but contains no dedicated identity data, and occasional misattribution appears, plausibly
from model-generated content in the web-scale pretraining data. The degeneration reports
feed back into the evaluation suite as an automated degeneration metric, an n-gram repetition ratio computed on the generations of a small set of benchmarks of varying difficulty.
Two further practices support it: every release candidate passes an automated compatibility suite of roughly 65 checks before distribution, covering prompt and begin-of-sequence
invariants, degeneration probes, multimodal inputs, reasoning-channel separation, and the
tool-calling and streaming paths; and we bisect behavioral regressions by serving every
post-training stage side by side and comparing them on the same prompts.

63

6

I NFRASTRUCTURE , S CALING , AND E FFICIENCY

Whereas the Apertus 1.0 report documents the Alps Research Infrastructure in greater
detail, this chapter focuses on what the 1.5 campaign adds. The introduction of new
modalities and an expanded vocabulary brings additional infrastructure costs, while asynchronous reinforcement learning introduces a new class of workload. These runs share a
production system whose scheduling environment, operating system, and storage infrastructure continue to evolve over the course of the project. We describe the platform and
its operation (Section 6.1), the throughput and interruption records of the training runs
(Section 6.2), their energy footprint (Section 6.3), and the engineering approaches used to
address the challenges encountered (Section 6.4).
6.1

I NFRASTRUCTURE

Both models are trained on the Alps supercomputer at CSCS using a flexible reservation
of GH200 nodes that grows from approximately 150 to 600 nodes as production ramps
up. This allocation is shared across the Apertus 1.5 training campaign and related project
workloads, including preparatory work for subsequent post-training stages. The production runs use 256 nodes for the 8B model and 512 nodes for the 70B model.
Alps, being a shared production system serving multiple users and projects, must also
accommodate unrelated workloads alongside the Apertus campaign. For approximately
two weeks in the middle of the project, one such full-scale workload requires the entire
machine. During this period, training continues in the available night and weekend reservation windows, with schedules adjusted at short notice to match system availability.
All training jobs run in containers based on NGC images and integrated with the Alps software and networking environment through site-provided mechanisms. Library versions
are pinned where required, and project-specific patches are applied on top of the validated
software stack. During the project, the cluster operating system and GPU driver are also
upgraded through staged rollouts: changes are first deployed to a small test partition and
subsequently rolled out cluster-wide.
The cluster has undergone no major changes since Apertus 1.0 and is documented in detail
there (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026).
6.2

F ULL T RAINING R UN P ERFORMANCE

CSCS engineering teams contribute specialized expertise in analyzing and optimizing the
performance of the Apertus workloads at scale. During the pretraining preparatory phase,
they profile the workloads across the system and apply targeted tuning to its network,
storage, memory, and compute components; this work continues as the runs scale up and
new bottlenecks emerge. In parallel, CSCS and the Apertus core team jointly tune the 70B
pretraining configuration, including sequence parallelism, virtual pipelining, and larger
gradient-communication buckets. Together with node-level memory and clock tuning,
these optimizations achieve roughly 750 tokens per second per GPU at 256 nodes, on par
with the Apertus 1.0 70B run despite the doubled sequence length and enlarged vocabulary. Scaling from 256 to 512 nodes retains roughly 90% parallel efficiency.
Table 40 reports the measured performance of the production phases, aggregated from
the logged run segments; each SLURM job opens a new segment, so the segment count
records how often scheduling windows and failures partition a phase. The 8B sustains
roughly 4,900 to 5,500 tokens per second per GPU (270 to 300 TFLOPs per GPU, a model
FLOPs utilization (MFU) of 0.27 to 0.30 against the nominal 989 TFLOPs bf16 peak of the
GH200, which understates efficiency on Alps, where each GH200 module runs under a
660 W power cap (Section 6.3) and cannot reach that peak), and the 70B 430 to 710 (0.20 to
0.33). The long-context extension pays the expected attention cost: throughput decreases
with each doubling of the context window, although cross-document attention masking
partially alleviates this degradation. Sustained rates overstate delivered progress: dividing
consumed tokens by segment wall time gives an effective rate roughly 30% lower for the
64

70B, the gap consisting of job startup, checkpoint saving and loading, and stalls before
failures.

counter; throughput and TFLOPs per GPU are means over the longest segment of each
phase; wall time sums the process time of all segments of the phase.
