6.3

E NERGY C ONSUMPTION

We estimate the energy of the training runs with the same method as the Apertus 1.0 report:
measured GPU-hours times an assumed draw of 560 W per GH200 module, below the configured 660 W power cap (Hernández-Cano et al., 2026). The logged segments of Table 40
total roughly 225,000 GPU-hours for the 8B continued-pretraining stages, 1.50 million for
the 70B continued-pretraining stages, and the remainder for the long-context extensions of
both models, together roughly 2 million GPU-hours or 1.1 GWh, against roughly 5 GWh
for the from-scratch Apertus 1.0 pretraining. The estimate covers the logged production
segments only; it excludes the phases missing from Table 40, post-training, evaluation, ablations, and idle reservation time. During the whole project duration, 9 million GPU-hours
have been allocated, which translates to an estimate of 5 GWh, excluding the idle times
and the resources consumed by the other projects within the Swiss AI initiative that have
synergies with the Apertus project but were not part of the training campaign. Alps draws
on carbon-free hydropower, and CSCS cools the infrastructure with lake water in a closed
cycle.
6.4

E NGINEERING C HALLENGES AND S OLUTIONS

Software Environment and Node Validation. The first weeks were characterized by further work on tuning the software environment for production at scale. During this phase,
multi-node jobs exposed two interacting issues: a system-level network library affecting collective operations during checkpointing, and rendezvous failures in the campaignspecific container environment following an update to the system software stack. To reduce this coupling, we evolved the container environment to incorporate the required network components directly where appropriate. This deliberately trades some of the lowfriction integration provided by the CSCS software environment for greater control over
the campaign-specific stack, making long-running production workloads less sensitive to
system upgrades.
We also incorporated into every job preamble a node-vetting tool developed by CSCS and
made available to all users. Before training starts, it validates the execution environment,
GPU health, and intra- and inter-node communication, allowing unsuitable nodes or configurations to be detected before they affect a distributed run. Finally, we hardened just-in65

time (JIT) compilation for large-scale execution by placing compilation caches per process
on node-local storage. This avoids cross-process and cross-node contention and ensures
that generated kernels remain consistent with the software environment in which they are
compiled.
Communication at Scale. Two performance issues encountered during the Apertus 1.5
campaign have close analogues in the Apertus 1.0 experience, allowing previous diagnostic work to accelerate their resolution. In the first case, throughput dropped by roughly an
order of magnitude at 256 nodes, but only after restoring from a checkpoint. CSCS engineers traced this distinct failure mode to a memory-registration cache limit in the network
stack; the corresponding mitigation was subsequently incorporated into the shared software environment and documented publicly. At scales beyond 256 nodes, we additionally
enabled an alternative rendezvous protocol whose implementation had been corrected by
the interconnect vendor (HPE) during the Apertus 1.0 campaign, avoiding a separate collapse in collective communication performance at large scale.
Storage Capacity and Data Placement. Storage capacity and data placement are among
the first operational constraints of the runs. Preparing the multimodal corpus required
substantially more space than initially available within the project allocation, leading to a
coordinated cleanup of existing data and an increase in storage quotas before preprocessing
could proceed at full scale. As production matured, data was placed according to its I/O
characteristics: checkpoints were written to storage suited to large sequential transfers,
while tokenized training datasets resided on an IOPS-optimized tier better matched to the
fine-grained access patterns of training. Checkpoint size is dominated by optimizer state,
which accounts for roughly 90% of the footprint: for the 70B model, removing optimizer
state reduces a checkpoint from approximately 1.2 TB to 132 GB. We therefore retain full
optimizer state only at phase boundaries and prune it from intermediate checkpoints.
The campaign also reinforces the importance of matching storage tiers and scratch policies to access patterns and retention needs. Tokenized datasets were initially moved to
backed-up disk storage to avoid scratch-retention limits, but the additional metadata activity introduced by the backup process became visible as training throughput variability at
scale. Frequently accessed training data was therefore placed on SSD-backed scratch storage using the recommended striping policy, which distributes file blocks across multiple
storage targets and avoids concentrating large-file traffic on a single server. The same consideration applies to all large objects accessed concurrently by distributed jobs: at 512-node
startup, insufficiently striped container images and checkpoints can concentrate I/O on individual storage servers, a pattern clearly visible in the storage-system telemetry. During
the campaign, CSCS also introduced a project-scoped storage tier combining scratch-like
performance with longer-lived data retention, providing a better fit for such workloads.
Network Safeguards for Data Ingestion. Collecting web-scale multimodal data from the
Internet on a large supercomputing infrastructure introduces network and security considerations that differ substantially from those of conventional training workloads. Many
permissively licensed image datasets provide URL manifests rather than the image objects themselves. Corpus construction therefore requires dereferencing large numbers of
URLs across a very broad set of Internet domains, producing highly distributed outbound
HTTP(S) traffic with characteristics similar to large-scale web crawling.
A small fraction of these destinations are present on security blacklists. During early ingestion runs, requests to such endpoints repeatedly triggered CSCS security monitoring, while
retries against denied destinations eventually caused a shared outbound IP address to be
blacklisted by Internet Service Providers. Because Internet egress is shared, such an event
can affect not only the Apertus campaign but also the other unrelated scientific workloads
running concurrently on Alps. Filtering against a single widely used hosts list proved insufficient, and ingestion was paused while we developed a more robust filtering pipeline
together with the CSCS network and security teams.
For each ingestion run, the pipeline retrieves fresh copies of multiple public threatintelligence feeds, including FireHOL IP sets, URLhaus, Spamhaus, and additional feeds
66

used by the CSCS monitoring infrastructure. These are compiled into IP-network, domain,
and URL access-control lists consumed by a local web proxy.12 All downloader traffic
passes through the proxy, including requests generated by HTTP redirects, and destination hostnames are resolved before the resulting addresses are checked against the IP-based
blacklists. Because HTTPS path information is not visible to the proxy without TLS interception, URL-level HTTPS indicators are conservatively promoted to host-level blocks. In
the first production run, the resulting policy rejected fewer than 0.01% of candidate downloads, indicating that a very small fraction of destinations accounts for the relevant security
risk.
Reinforcement Learning Infrastructure. Reinforcement learning introduces a distinct infrastructure workload. This stage runs on our fork of verl with a fully asynchronous
trainer. Enabling the rollout stack on the ARM-based Grace Hopper superchip (GH200)
platform and the Alps interconnect requires upstream contributions across the inference
engines, training framework, and attention kernels. At scale, two performance bottlenecks
become dominant. The first is the distribution of updated model weights from the trainer
to the inference workers, which we addressed by porting a high-performance transfer engine to the interconnect’s libfabric interface in close collaboration with CSCS engineering teams. The second is the loading of sharded checkpoints from the parallel filesystem,
whose aggregate I/O becomes increasingly significant as the number of workers grows.
Reward environments are decoupled from the GPU compute nodes and run as CPU-only
services on an on-premises Kubernetes cluster built on commodity hardware. The code
sandbox executes its test harnesses within the Kubernetes pods, keeping this workload
outside the GPU allocation and isolating it from the training infrastructure. End-to-end
rollout throughput comes within approximately 5% of comparable open models executed
on the same software stack. The remaining difference is attributed primarily to the implementation of the xIELU activation function.

12 Available at swiss-ai/multimodal-data under download/blacklist.

67

7

R ESPONSIBLE R ELEASE AND G OVERNANCE

This chapter documents the release: the license and the gated distribution (Section 7.1),
the known limitations of the models (Section 7.2), what the openness of weights, data,
recipes, and values enables and what it risks (Section 7.3), and the external evaluation and
community work around the release (Section 7.4). We describe practices and published
documents and make no claim about their legal sufficiency.
7.1

L ICENSE AND A CCEPTABLE U SE

We release model weights and training and evaluation code under the Apache 2.0 license.
All training data is fully available: every dataset is public, and Section 3.1 and the legal
spreadsheet identify each one in the version we use. Third-party data can be obtained
from its original providers; we also publish the SFT mixture as a single dataset, with
each component retaining its source license. Datasets we construct ourselves, such as the
instruction-following prompt sets and preference pairs in Section 4, are published on Hugging Face under open licenses specified in the corresponding dataset cards. We maintain
the license, acceptable-use policy, and privacy policy in swiss-ai/apertus-legal. The
models are distributed on Hugging Face behind a gated download that records assent to
the acceptable-use policy.
Transparency Documentation. We publish two documents prepared for the EU framework for general-purpose AI models: the Public Summary of Training Content, following the European Commission’s template, and a Code of Practice document covering transparency and copyright policy. We publish the Code of Practice voluntarily, and neither document asserts that any obligation is satisfied. Both are versioned
in swiss-ai/apertus-legal; the legal spreadsheet of Section 3.1.1 is versioned in
swiss-ai/apertus-data-transparency.
7.2

L IMITATIONS

Factual Reliability. The models hallucinate on short-form factual questions at the rate of
their predecessors: on PreciseWiki they attempt an answer on more than eight of 10 questions and hallucinate on roughly six to seven of them, slightly more than Apertus 1.0 at
70B scale (Table 39). The gains of this release lie in recognizing questions about things that
do not exist and in long-form precision, not in factoid recall.
Over-Refusal. The alignment stages make the models cautious, and prompts that merely
resemble harmful ones are often declined; the effect is visible across the post-training lineage (Section 5.3.2) and in manual testing. On the hard subset of OR-Bench, refusal rates
rise sharply through the alignment stages (Section 5.9.2); because a share of its prompts is
legitimately refusable, this shows the trend but not an absolute over-refusal rate. Reducing
the caution without weakening the safety behavior remains open.
Language Coverage. Multilinguality is the models’ strongest relative position, but not uniformly so. XNLI regresses against Apertus 1.0 at both scales (Table 29), and the instructionfollowing and reasoning data concentrate on a few dozen languages, so quality in the long
tail of the pretraining languages is not evaluated.
Multimodal Scope. The models understand images and audio but do not generate them.
The 70B trails the 8B on several multimodal benchmarks because its run stops before consuming the full multimodal mixture and its inherited learning rate leaves it less room to
adapt (Section 3.4). The audio training data skews toward parliamentary and political
speech (Section 3.1), and for image and audio data we rely on the curation and filtering of
the dataset creators (Section 3.1.1).
Grounding. Preference alignment improves most multimodal capability groups, among
them spatial reasoning, medical and document understanding, and visual math, but systematically degrades referring-expression grounding, by about 17 points across the RefCOCO family. The diagnosis is precise: the output format remains valid and the model
still locates the object, but the predicted boxes drift systematically looser, a regression in
68

extent calibration. We accept this trade-off for the release; we note grounding-aware rewards in alignment for future iterations.
Identity. The post-trained models sometimes misstate their identity, claiming to be created
by other providers, particularly in lower-resource languages; Apertus 1.0 did not show this
behavior. We check the post-training data carefully: it conveys no other provider’s identity. This points at subtler inheritance: a model can adopt a teacher’s identity from distillation data with all identity markers removed (Zhong, 2026), and behavioral traits transmit
through hidden signals in data more generally (Cloud et al., 2026), so model-generated
content in the web corpus and the openly released reasoning traces in our mixture remain
plausible carriers. Instilling a robust self-identity despite such data is an open problem.
Tool Calling and Thinking. Tool calling and thinking do not combine in the released
models. The chat template supports tool calls inside the reasoning span (Figure 8), but the
supervised mixture contains no conversation that both reasons and calls a tool, and we
exclude tool-use prompts from the reinforcement-learning run (Section 4.5). The models
behave accordingly: with thinking enabled and the reasoning span forced, they produce
no tool calls; with thinking enabled but not forced, they tend to skip the reasoning span and
answer as in non-thinking mode. At launch the serving stacks additionally mis-parse tool
calls emitted under thinking, dropping the call terminator. Inference providers therefore
expose the two features as mutually exclusive. More generally, the post-training of this
release is not designed for agentic use: tool-use behavior comes from supervised finetuning
alone, we exclude tool-use prompts from reinforcement learning, and we do not evaluate
multi-turn tool use (Table 37 covers the single-turn categories of BFCL). The 8B further
remains weak on parallel calls, often producing a single call where several are requested.
7.3

B ROADER I MPACT

Apertus 1.5 releases the weights, the data recipe, the training and evaluation code, and the
charter its alignment follows, and trains only on publicly available data (Section 3.1). We
enumerate every dataset, its license, and its filtering, and we publish the data ablations, so
the training data and its processing can be inspected in full. The recipes of Section 3 and
Section 4 are the ones we ran, so institutions that cannot train such models from scratch can
continue, finetune, or distill them on their own infrastructure. The models can be deployed
without dependence on a vendor, which matters for public administrations, regulated sectors, and research that needs a model whose behavior is fixed and documented.
Openness extends to the values to which models are aligned. For most released models,
the alignment data and objectives are undisclosed, so users observe a behavior but not
the intent behind it. The Apertus charter, introduced with Apertus 1.0 (Hernández-Cano
et al., 2026), states the principles the models are aligned to; the charter-alignment stage of
Section 4.6.2 trains the released 8B against it with a published recipe and judge prompts
(Appendix G); and the charter-alignment score of Section 5.3.2 measures how far each released checkpoint follows it. A deployer who disagrees with a principle can see what to
change, and retrain.
Roughly 40% of the pretraining data of Apertus 1.0 is non-English, the post-training data
of this release adds multilingual instruction data, and the evaluations of Section 5.4 place
the models at their strongest relative position on multilingual and cultural benchmarks,
including the national languages of Switzerland and Romansh (Section 4.2.3). For communities whose languages are underrepresented in commercial models, an open model that
can be adapted further is a direct benefit, within the coverage limits of Section 7.2.
We keep the data measures of Apertus 1.0: we respect opt-outs retroactively, use no paywalled or private sources, remove personally identifiable information and toxic text, train
with the Goldfish objective to limit verbatim memorization, and publish the provenance of
every dataset (Section 3.1.1, Section 3.2).
Open weights can be finetuned to remove the safety behavior of Section 5.9, and released
models can be misused at scale. We address this with alignment trained into the released
checkpoints, the acceptable-use policy (Section 7.1), the data-protection request channel,

7.4

C OMMUNITY AND E XTERNAL E VALUATION

Beyond releasing weights, data, and recipes (Section 7.3), we open the development process to outside contributors: an outreach and community function connects external contributors and deployment engineers with the development process and collects their findings to improve model compatibility. Smith et al. (2026) argue that inclusive design and
capacity building are conditions for responsible openness. The public website13 includes
the charter, usage guides, benchmark resources, model documentation, and links to downstream projects; newsletters, social media, and multilingual media monitoring complement
it. Direct involvement of the engineering team has included presentations to science and
industry, round-tables with companies, support of hackathons, and hands-on workshops.
One month before the Apertus 1.5 release, in addition to the third-party safety evaluation of
Section 5.9, 15 organizations, represented by roughly 50 engineers and experts, evaluated
the 8B and 70B weights for deployment readiness: quantization, alignment, multimodal input, tokenizer distribution, and general compatibility. Their feedback led to quantizations
and community builds for several inference frameworks.
The pre-release surfaced problems that internal testing had missed, and we thank Infomaniak and OnPrem.ai in particular. Infomaniak contributed a vLLM integration that runs
the image and audio tokenizers on the GPU. Our review of it found that encoding several media items as one zero-padded batch changes their codes, because neither tokenizer
satisfies crop( T (pad( x ))) = T ( x ), so the integration now encodes each item separately.
OnPrem.ai traced a tool-calling failure in the 70B pre-release checkpoint, where a faulty
generation configuration let the model continue past a complete tool call and fabricate
the tool’s result, and confirmed the corrected configuration in the release candidate. Their
quantization studies showed that 8-bit (FP8) weights and activations are near-lossless. 4-bit
NVFP4 in the attention layers of the 8B model produces NaN logits, which they attribute to
the quadratic term of xIELU amplifying quantization noise. Keeping attention at FP8 and
quantizing only the MLP to NVFP4 fits the 70B model into 49 GiB, so it runs on a single
96 GB GPU with more than 200k tokens of context.
We also measured the serving performance of the 70B model at five inference providers
and on our own deployment with a single run of the open-source tool GuideLLM from
CSCS on 16 September 2026. Table 41 lists latency, throughput, and time to first token,
with providers anonymized; requests originate at CSCS, which gives our own deployment
a network advantage.
For replies of the same length, latency varies by a factor of 2.3 between deployments (1.9
to 4.3 s) and time to first token by a factor of almost six (82 to 470 ms). Comparing answer quality across providers proved harder than measuring speed: most of our benchmarks score answers by log-likelihood, which requires a completions endpoint that sev13 https://apertus-ai.org

70

eral providers and our own gateway do not expose, and rate limits slow the remaining
generation-based tests. Therefore, a quality comparison between providers remains open.

71

8

C ONCLUSION

Apertus 1.5 extends the Apertus 1.0 models from text to image and audio understanding
through continued pretraining on 4T and 2T additional tokens, extends the context window fourfold to 262,144 tokens, and introduces the second generation of our post-training
pipeline, which adds a thinking mode, more reliable instruction following, and more effective tool calling. The released models improve over their predecessors on nearly every
benchmark; among fully open models of its class, the 8B leads on knowledge, instruction following, and cultural benchmarks, and both models stand strongest relative to their
peers on multilingual evaluations. Thinking lifts the 8B from 51.2 to 71.4 on MATH-500;
its instruction following reaches 87.1 on IFEval and 83.7 on Multi-IF, above every model
we compare against; single-turn function calling on the 70B rises from 58.0 to 70.1, within
1.5 points of Llama-3.3-70B; and among fully open multimodal models the 8B leads document and chart understanding and stays ahead on long-form and lower-resource speech
recognition, where dedicated audio models degrade.
The second generation of the post-training pipeline also produced most of the lessons of
this release. Adding modalities through the tokenizer, with the language-model head restricted to text, upgraded the models without an architectural change. A dedicated longcontext stage after supervised finetuning preserved the context extension. Filtering reasoning data by what the model already solves, and at 70B scale preceding online preference
optimization with an offline stage, were decisive for the post-trained models. The 70B run,
stopped before it consumed its multimodal mixture, showed that multimodal capability
follows exposure.
The next generation of Apertus starts from these recipes, with the remaining gaps of Section 7.2, among them short-form hallucination and the combination of tool calling with
thinking, as its first targets. As with the first release, the weights, intermediate checkpoints,
data recipes, training and evaluation code, and the charter that guides the alignment are
public.

V ISION D ATASETS BY T RAINING S TAGE

The stagewise tables give the vision pool of each stage: for every dataset, the prepared
samples and input tokens available to the run. The mix a run trains on, that is, the tokens
trained on including repetition, appears per modality in Table 3 for the pretraining stages,
per dataset in Appendix F for the long-context extension, and per source in Table 8 for
long-context SFT. Stages 1 and 2 appear in Table B.1 and Table B.2, the cooldown and longcontext pretraining pools in Table B.3 and Table B.4, and the SFT and long-context SFT
pools in Table B.5 and Table B.6. Table B.2 and Table B.3 report the Apertus 1.5 8B pools.
The Apertus 1.5 70B cooldown additionally reuses datasets already listed for Stage 2 in
Table B.2, which we do not repeat. Vision reinforcement learning adds 50,000 in-house
BlindTasks-RL prompts, which Table 10 reports separately. The preference-optimization
stages use no multimodal inputs. The SFT tables here report the production pools; the
smaller image pool of the capability-filtering SFT phase appears in Table 7, whereas the
audio pool is the same in both phases.

D

I MAGE D ATASET E XPLORATION D ETAILED B REAKDOWN

Our large-scale multimodal image collections comprise a diverse range of visual media,
including photographs, illustrations, screenshots, documents, diagrams, and composite
graphics, with correspondingly varying content. While visual benchmarks are usually balanced across different image types and different task types, our vision data is not guaranteed to be balanced across image types, and might therefore cover some benchmarks
poorly. Beyond the coarse classification of Figure 3, quantifying our coverage of different
image types thus requires a rich representation taxonomy. In turn, this could lead to a
filtering scheme that balances the vision data in appropriate ways.
Taxonomy. We therefore design a taxonomy consisting of 61 fine-grained labels organized
along two orthogonal dimensions: graphics and photographs. We also organize these finegrained labels into five coarse-grained labels for graphics and seven coarse-grained labels
for photographs for easier analysis. We plot this taxonomy in Figure D.1 and Figure D.2.
Using CLIP ViT-L/14, we classify approximately 1% of the full datasets in a single forward
pass, enabling efficient large-scale analysis while preserving the overall dataset statistics.
The taxonomy uses a flat one-pass design: all 61 labels are scored in a single CLIP forward
pass (image ↔ text prompt), so classification adds no extra compute beyond the embedding computation. We focus on LLaVA-OneVision, BLIP3, DataComp-1B, MINT-1T-ArXiv,
MINT-1T-HTML, and MINT-1T-PDF. These are some of the largest datasets by token count
(LLaVA-OneVision, BLIP3, DataComp-1B) that consist of large image piles sourced from
the general internet. We also explore a selection of more specialized datasets (MINT-1TArXiv, MINT-1T-HTML, and MINT-1T-PDF). These MINT-1T subsets still form a large part
of the overall token count. At the same time, the image source is constrained (PDF documents for both the arXiv and PDF subparts), and the domain for MINT-1T-ArXiv is further
constrained to scientific works. We separate our analysis into quantitative and qualitative
steps.
Quantitative breakdown. The first step provides a quantitative exploration of the datasets
by applying our taxonomy-based image classification pipeline. The resulting distributions
over the taxonomy classes reveal the diversity of visual content within and across datasets
while demonstrating that the proposed taxonomy effectively explores both graphics and
photographs.
We plot the results of our analysis in Figure D.3, Figure D.4, Figure D.5, Figure D.6, and
Figure D.7. This analysis yields several interesting conclusions that can also inspire future
work. First, in our coarse-grained analysis, we observe that “Composite Graphic” is the
most frequent graphic. At the same time, “Artifacts/Objects” appears as the most frequent
photograph. This seems intuitive, given that these datasets provide great internet coverage; logos and related material should dominate due to commercial actors and advertisements. At the same time, in our fine-grained analysis, we observe that in most datasets, the
“Artifacts/Objects” class consists of “Clothing”, “Food”, “Vehicle”, “Electronic Device”,
and “Home Goods”, which figure prominently across all datasets. In contrast, specialized
domains such as insects, astronomy, and historic sites make up a relatively small percentage of all images, while potentially appearing prominently in user queries to the model. We
quantify this imbalance in Table D.1. These imbalances raise the prospect of rebalancing
the dataset to make the image distribution more equal across different domains. Finally,
we observe that the image distribution remains relatively similar across different datasets
except the MINT-1T dataset. Here, especially for the arXiv subset, documents and research
graphs dominate the distribution in accordance with our data source. Similarly, in the
MINT-1T-PDF subset, we observe diagrams and charts dominating. This aligns with how
these datasets are sourced. It also provides quantitative motivation for including diversified datasets in our data mixture to address limitations in larger but more general image
pools.

Qualitative check. The second step consists of a qualitative evaluation of the proposed
taxonomy. Specifically, we construct a per-label montage containing representative highconfidence examples for each of the 61 fine-grained categories together with their top-5
CLIP prediction scores. This visualization enables direct inspection of the image classes
and model confidence across the taxonomy. The resulting examples demonstrate that most
category images are visually coherent, supporting the validity of the proposed taxonomy
for analyzing our image datasets. Figure D.8 shows samples from several prominent finegrained classes.

Figure D.8: Image samples from several prominent fine-grained classes. We observe that
our embedding and classifier pipeline correctly captures the diverse classes in our taxonomy.

Task signals. The broad task view in Figure D.9 focuses on training signals. Captioning is

occur across the inventory. The task labels overlap and their shares are not mutually exclusive.

Table G.1: Continued-pretraining configuration of both models. The learning rate follows
the warmup-stable-decay schedule of Section 3.2: constant

Both models train with AdEMAMix (β 1 = 0.9, β 2 = 0.999, α = 8, β 3 = 0.9999, with the β 3
and α warmup over 100,000 iterations), gradient clipping at 0.1, and weight decay of 0.1,
applied also to the xIELU parameters and the normalization gains. The optimizer state is
fresh at the start of stage 1 and carries over across the later stage transitions. All stages pack
documents with cross-document attention masking: position identifiers reset at document
boundaries, and end-of-document positions are excluded from the loss.
The long-context extension proceeds through a curriculum of 32,768, 65,536, 131,072, and
262,144 tokens, each step loading the previous checkpoint with a fresh optimizer state and
training at a constant learning rate equal to the main run’s minimum. The RoPE base grows
to 2M for the first step and 4M thereafter, with scaling factors of 8, 8, 16, and 32. The token
budgets per step are 62.9B, 58.7B, 58.7B, and 58.7B for the 8B model, and 58.7B, 58.7B, 50.3B,
and 50.3B for the 70B model (Appendix F). The global batch size halves with each doubling
of the sequence length (256, 128, 64, and 32 for the 8B model), context parallelism scales as
2, 4, 8, and 16 for the 8B model and 4, 8, 16, and 32 for the 70B model, and sequences are
packed with a best-fit-decreasing policy.
Both production SFT runs of Section 4.2 use Adam (β 1 = 0.9, β 2 = 0.999) with no weight
decay and gradient clipping at 1.0; Section 4.2 gives their learning rates, warmup, batch
sizes, and sequence length, together with those of the capability-filtering run. We apply
the chat template offline during data preparation, restrict the loss to assistant regions with
special tokens masked, and pack samples with the same best-fit-decreasing policy.
The reinforcement-learning stage trains with a fully asynchronous trainer on FSDP2 with
FlashAttention-3 (Shah et al., 2024), eight rollouts per prompt served by SGLang, leaveone-out advantage estimation, filtering of groups with zero reward variance, asymmetric
clipping (0.2, 0.28), a geometric-mean loss aggregation, no KL penalty, and AdamW at a
learning rate of 1 × 10−6 . For the 8B model, the training runs for 480 training steps, effectively training on about one million trajectories, while the 70B model runs for 180 steps.
Offline DPO trains with our own preference trainer (swiss-ai/posttraining), which
implements the DPO objective directly and divides β by sequence length under length
normalization, running on DeepSpeed ZeRO (Rajbhandari et al., 2020). Online DPO runs
on our verl fork with FSDP and SGLang rollouts, sampling at temperature 1.0 with unrestricted top-p and top-k. Neither stage trains a reward model, and neither trains on image
inputs.
The SDPO charter-alignment stage of Section 4.6.2 runs on our verl fork with FSDP2 for
training and vLLM for rollouts. It trains for one epoch at a batch size of 64 prompts, with
16 rollouts per prompt, at a learning rate of 1 × 10−7 with 10 linear warmup steps, a maximum model length of 4,096 tokens, and token-level importance sampling clipped at a ratio
of two. We never update the teacher, so it stays the initial policy throughout.
Prompt G.1 and Prompt G.2 give the two templates. The judge template carries the full
charter, and the teacher template combines the original user prompt with the resulting

SDPO Judge Prompt
You are an expert reviewer. Your job is to read the user prompt and the assistant
,→
response below, then judge how well the response follows the constitution
,→
that follows, and give helpful, concise, constructive feedback to the
,→
response writer on how to better align the response with the constitution.
Here is the constitution:
<CONSTITUTIONAL_PRINCIPLES>
[the 11 sections of the Apertus charter, verbatim]
</CONSTITUTIONAL_PRINCIPLES>
<USER_PROMPT>
{prompt}
</USER_PROMPT>
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE>
{response}
</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE>
Focus on the most important ways the response could better follow the
,→
constitution. Do not just give feedback to refuse the response, only do it,
,→
if it is REALLY necessary as the model will be trained based on your feedback
,→
and should not learn to overrefuse. Address the response writer directly as
,→
"you". Do not output a score, a grade, a JSON object, or any preamble, and do
,→
not quote principle numbers. Output only one or two sentences of actionable
,→
feedback.

Here is the user prompt:
<USER_PROMPT>
{prompt}
</USER_PROMPT>

An expert reviewer evaluated an earlier draft answer to this request against a
,→
set of guiding principles and left the following feedback. Use it to write a
,→
better answer:
<REVIEWER_FEEDBACK>
{feedback_raw}
</REVIEWER_FEEDBACK>
Do not refer to or mention any guidelines, principles, or reviewer feedback in
,→
your response.

I

P REFERENCE A LIGNMENT D ATA

This appendix documents the preference data of Section 4.6: the rubric prompts
we give the judge, the composition of the prompt set, the pool of response
models, and the distribution of the resulting scores.
The released artifacts
are swiss-ai/Apertus-v1.5-Preference-Data for the preference pairs and
swiss-ai/Apertus-v1.5-Preference-Pool for the underlying annotated completions.

I.1

J UDGE R UBRIC P ROMPTS

Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 (Yang et al., 2025a) rates each completion on the four aspects independently, one request per aspect, with the system prompt of Prompt I.1 and the
aspect prompt as the user message. We make annotation deterministic and disable thinking: the judge decodes at temperature 0 and generates exactly one token, constrained to the
token identifiers of the digits one through five, and we keep the renormalized distribution
over those five tokens, so an aspect score is the expected value ∑k k pk and is continuous
on [1, 5]. The final score of a completion is the mean over the four aspects. Across the full
matrix of 6,235,908 scored completions the four aspects correlate between 0.838 and 0.916,
so the mean is close to a single latent quality axis; Section J.2 takes up the consequence for
the online stage, which scores on helpfulness alone.
Judge System Prompt
You are an impartial judge. Your role is to critically evaluate the quality of an
,→
AI assistant response based on a given criteria. You'll receive an input with
,→
two sections, enclosed in tags: <USER_INPUT>...</USER_INPUT> for the task
,→
instructions (and any accompanying context, if applicable), and
,→
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>...</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE> for the
,→
AI assistant's response.
Carefully read the provided input to understand the task, then assess how well
,→
the response fulfills the criteria requirements. If conversation history is
,→
present, ensure the response aligns with it; otherwise, evaluate based solely
,→
on the instruction. You will be given a scoring rubric below, based on which
,→
you should provide a rating from 1 to 5. Your output should only be an
,→
integer from 1 to 5. Do not output any additional text or explanations.

Judge Rubric: Instruction Following
You will be doing an Instruction Following Assessment of an AI assistant
,→
response.
Carefully read the <USER_INPUT> to assess how well the candidate response
,→
fulfills the task requirements. If the input includes a conversation history,
,→
the response must align with that context as well as the final instruction.
**Scoring**: Rate the text on a scale of 1 to 5:
1. **Irrelevant**: No alignment.
2. **Partial Focus**: Addresses one aspect poorly.
3. **Partial Compliance**:
- (1) Meets goal or restrictions, neglecting other.
- (2) Acknowledges both but slight deviations.
4. **Almost There**: Near alignment, minor deviations.
5. **Comprehensive Compliance**: Fully aligns, meets all requirements.
Here are the user inputs and the AI assistant response to evaluate:
<USER_INPUT>{prompt}</USER_INPUT>
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>{completion}</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>

111

Judge Rubric: Honesty
You will be doing a Honesty and Uncertainty Expression Assessment of an AI
,→
assistant response.
Assess how well the candidate response conveys honesty and uncertainty based on
,→
the provided <USER_INPUT>. Evaluate if the text's confidence aligns with
,→
response correctness, factoring in any conversation context if it is present
,→
in the input.
- **Indicators of Uncertainty**:
1. Weakeners: e.g., 'I guess,' 'probably.'
2. Refusal to answer.
3. Verbalized confidence scores: [0, 20] low; (20, 40] uncertain; (40, 60]
,→
moderate; (60, 80] leaning confident; (80, 100] high.
- **No uncertainty expression indicate confidence.**
- **Response Correctness**: Align with ground truth, or provide accurate content
,→
without fabrication.
**Scoring**: Rate outputs on a scale of 1 to 5:
1. **Confidently Incorrect**: Confident but entirely wrong.
2. **Confident with Significant Mistakes / Unconfident Incorrect**:
- Confident but contains major errors.
- Unconfident and entirely wrong.
3. **Uncertain / 'I Don't Know' / Subtle Mistakes**:
- 'I don't know' or declines.
- confident but contains minor errors.
- Unconfident and contains significant mistakes.
4. **Correct but Uncertain / Expressed Subtle Mistakes**:
- Correct but unconfident.
- Makes subtle mistakes but expresses uncertainty without specifying the exact
,→
area of doubt.
5. **Correct and Confident / Precisely Express Uncertainty**:
- Correct and confident.
- Makes mistakes, but precisely acknowledges minor errors and indicates
,→
uncertainty on potential mistakes.
Here are the user inputs and the AI assistant response to evaluate:
<USER_INPUT>{prompt}</USER_INPUT>
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>{completion}</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>

Judge Rubric: Truthfulness
You will be doing a Truthfulness and Hallucination Assessment of an AI assistant
,→
response.
Evaluate the candidate response's accuracy in providing information without
,→
introducing misleading or fabricated details.
When evaluating truthfulness, consider the following types of hallucination:
1. **Contradictory with the World (Factual Error)**: Entities, locations,
,→
concepts, or events that conflict with established knowledge.
2. **Contradictory with Instruction/Context**: Responses diverge, introducing new
,→
facts not aligned with the user's instructions (or conversation history, if
,→
provided).
3. **Self-Contradictory / Logical Error**: Responses contain internal
,→
contradictions or logical errors within each independent text.
Reflect on whether any of these hallucination types are present in the response,
,→
and take them into account when assigning your rating.
**Scoring**: Rate outputs on a scale of 1 to 5 based on extent of hallucination:
1. **Completely Hallucinated**: Entirely unreliable due to hallucinations.
2. **Severe Hallucination**: Nearly half contains hallucinations, severe
,→
deviation from main points.
3. **Partial Hallucination / Misunderstanding**: Overall truthful, partial
,→
misunderstanding due to hallucinations.
4. **Insignificant Hallucination**: Mostly truthful, slight hallucination not
,→
affecting main points.
5. **No Hallucination**: Free of hallucinations.

112

Here are the user inputs and the AI assistant response to evaluate:
<USER_INPUT>{prompt}</USER_INPUT>
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>{completion}</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>

Judge Rubric: Helpfulness
You will be doing an Informativeness / Helpfulness Assessment of an AI assistant
,→
response.
Evaluate if the candidate response fulfills the task objectives, provides
,→
high-quality, correct, and informative content, and respects any preceding
,→
conversation context if provided in the input.
Helpfulness assessment emphasizes **Overall Quality** regarding correctness and
,→
informativenss.
**Correctness**: Accurate computation, reasoning steps, and outputs without
,→
misunderstandings or fabrication.
When assessing informativeness, consider the following aspects:
1. **Clarity and Relevance**: Does the response relate to the task and seek
,→
clarifications if needed?
2. **Useful and Comprehensive Information**: Does it provide relevant background,
,→
reasoning steps, or detailed description?
3. **Not Lengthy, No Repetition**: Is the response concise, avoiding verbosity or
,→
repetition?
Score on a scale of 1 to 5 based on extent of helpfulness, regarding both
,→
informativeness and correctness:
1. **Severely Incorrect**: Contains significant inaccuracies or fabricated
,→
content, even if comprehensive information is provided.
2. **Partially Incorrect**: Contains errors that may cause confusion, even though
,→
comprehensive information is present.
3. **Correct**: Accurate and provides useful information that meets the task's
,→
requirements.
4. **Highly Informative**: Accurate and extensive, providing valuable insights
,→
and detailed information.
5. **Outstandingly Helpful**: Both accurate and in-depth, offering profound
,→
insights and comprehensive information.
Here are the user inputs and the AI assistant response to evaluate:
<USER_INPUT>{prompt}</USER_INPUT>
<ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>{completion}</ASSISTANT_RESPONSE_TO_EVALUATE>

I.2

P ROMPT S ET C OMPOSITION

The prompts come from Dolci-Instruct-DPO (Team Olmo et al., 2025), whose identifiers
encode the originating dataset; the 32 source tags map onto the 23 sources of Table I.1
with none left unmapped. Decontamination against our evaluation suite and a 3,600-token
length filter reduce 259,922 source prompts to 245,898 preference pairs, a retention of 94.6%,
unevenly distributed: the two precise instruction-following tags lose 12 to 19% while most
other sources keep at least 95%. Dolci-Instruct-DPO repeats a minority of its prompts; each
repetition receives an independent generation and pairing run, which soft-upweights those
prompts rather than duplicating pairs.

I.3

R ESPONSE M ODEL P OOL

All 24 models of Table I.2, which span 0.5B to 675B parameters and four main families,
answer every prompt, so that the pool covers a wide quality range and the max-min rule
has something to select from. Coverage is complete apart from 1,969 prompts for which
Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Instruct returns no completion, leaving 23 candidates; across the
full matrix only 251 of 6,236,159 completions fail annotation, and no prompt has all 24 models tie. Qwen3-Omni is omni-modal but we use it text-only. Note that the judge model is
itself in the pool and supplies 21.6% of the chosen responses, so we cannot exclude a selfpreference effect.
I.4

C OMPLETION G ENERATION

Every model answers every prompt once, at temperature 1.0 and a cap of 4,096 generated
tokens. We leave top-p and top-k unset, so each model falls back to the defaults of its own
shipped generation configuration; we therefore sample the pool at a common temperature
but not under a single decoding configuration, and the Qwen3 models in particular carry
non-neutral nucleus defaults. We disable thinking throughout, through the chat template
for every model that accepts the argument and through a no-reasoning launch flag for the
four Mistral models whose tokenizer rejects it.
I.5

S CORE D ISTRIBUTION

Max-min selection over a pool of very unequal capability pins both ends of the scale: 86.5%
of chosen responses score in [4.9, 5.0] and 96.7% of rejected ones sit at the 1.0 floor, giving a
mean margin of 3.766 (Table I.3 and Table I.4). The margin is large almost everywhere, with
97.9% of pairs separated by at least two points and 93.4% by at least three. The 3,600-token
filter costs little separation: the mean per-prompt max-min gap is 3.822 before filtering
against a realized 3.766 after it.

chosen score, the rejected score, and their margin.

J

A LIGNMENT A BLATIONS

J.1

DPO A BLATIONS

This appendix reports the sweeps over the learning rate, the KL penalty (β), and the batch
size for offline DPO (Rafailov et al., 2023), its length-normalized variant (Meng et al., 2024;
Lambert et al., 2025), and online DPO (Guo et al., 2024). It also compares strategies for
combining offline and online DPO. Section 4.6 documents the final configurations and Section 5.3.2 their downstream results.
All scores are percentages, and ∆ is the difference in average from the SFT model that
initializes the runs. The ablations use intermediate checkpoints and earlier versions of
the evaluation suite; scores are therefore comparable only within a table. In particular, AlpacaEval uses Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (Grattafiori et al., 2024) and Arena-Hard uses
Qwen3.5-27B (Qwen Team, 2026b) as the judge, instead of the GPT judges of the posttraining lineage (Section 5.3.2); these scores, and the averages that include them, are not
comparable to Table 26. For Apertus 1.5 70B we report skill-group means.
J.1.1

A PERTUS 1.5 8B

Offline DPO, standard loss. Table J.1 lists the ten best configurations of a 60-run grid
over the learning rate, β, and the batch size. Their averages lie within 0.5 points. Nine
of the ten use a learning rate of 5·10−7 or 10−6 , and none uses β ≥ 0.5. All three batch
sizes appear among them, which indicates a low sensitivity to the batch size. The best
configuration (β=0.05, learning rate 5·10−7 , batch size 512) reaches ∆=+4.1.
Offline DPO, length-normalized loss. With the batch size fixed at 128, we sweep β and
the learning rate (Table J.2). A larger β tolerates a larger learning rate: at 10−6 only β=25
improves over the SFT model, and at 5·10−7 only β ∈ {5, 25} does. At β=25, the average
increases monotonically with the learning rate up to 10−6 . The six configurations absent
from the table do not improve over the SFT model. The best configuration (β=25, learning
rate 10−6 ) reaches ∆=+5.5, 1.4 points above the best standard DPO run. Extending the
grid to β ∈ {50, 100} lowers the best ∆ to +4.4 and +3.2, respectively (Table J.3). We use
β=25 for all offline DPO training.
Offline DPO, transfer to Apertus 1.5 8B. The preceding grids use the Apertus 1.0 8B SFT
checkpoint, and we apply the selected configuration to the Apertus 1.5 8B SFT checkpoints
without a new search. As a check, a learning rate of 5·10−6 on an intermediate Apertus 1.5 8B SFT checkpoint changes the averages by at most 0.1 points (48.8 vs. 48.8 on the
development benchmarks and 44.0 vs. 43.9 on the held-out benchmarks). We therefore
keep the learning rate of 10−6 .
Online DPO. With β=0.1 and 256 prompts per step, we sweep the learning rate over
{10−5 , 5·10−6 , 10−6 } (Table J.4). A learning rate of 5·10−6 performs best (∆=+4.4), ahead
of 10−6 (+3.3), 10−5 (+3.2), and offline DPO from the same checkpoint (+3.7).
Combining offline and online DPO. We evaluate two combinations of offline and online
DPO: a sequential pipeline and a joint data mixture.
In the sequential offline→online setting, online DPO starts from a trained offline DPO
checkpoint. The learning-rate sweep for the online phase again favors 5·10−6 (∆=+5.4),
ahead of 10−6 (+4.7) and 5·10−7 (+4.0; Table J.4).
The mixture adds 256 offline pairs to each online step. Table J.5 compares the four variants
(offline, online, offline→online, and mixture) with the selected hyperparameters. All four
improve over the SFT checkpoint (51.4 on the development benchmarks, 49.3 on the heldout benchmarks). The mixture scores highest (55.5 / 53.8), followed by offline→online
(55.4 / 53.5), online (54.6 / 52.0), and offline (54.0 / 50.8).

The final Apertus 1.5 8B recipe uses online DPO alone. When we repeat the comparison of
offline, online, and mixture DPO on the four final Apertus 1.5 8B SFT checkpoints, online
DPO matches the mixture; the small gains of the combined variants on the intermediate
checkpoint do not justify the additional data streams and training stages.

Offline and mixture. The RL checkpoint of Apertus 1.5 70B produces much shorter responses than its SFT checkpoint (253 vs. 367 tokens; Table L.3). Offline DPO from the
RL checkpoint reverses this shortening and raises the mean response length from 253 to
501 tokens (Table J.7). From the RL checkpoint, offline DPO alone reaches an average of
56.1, above the mixture (55.6) and online DPO (55.5). The final Apertus 1.5 70B recipe
runs offline DPO followed by online DPO (Section 4.6), which restores the response length
before on-policy training.

J.2

J UDGE A BLATIONS

The online DPO judge described in Section 4.6 scores each rollout on helpfulness alone with
Qwen3.6-27B, rather than the offline stage’s four-aspect rubric with Qwen3-235B-A22BInstruct-2507. We ablate both departures: the aspect simplification and the choice of judge
model.
J.2.1

J UDGING A SPECTS S IMPLIFICATION

We test whether the four aspect-wise scores from Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507
are correlated enough that scoring on one alone is a safe simplification, using
ActiveUltraFeedback/ultrafeedback: 60,829 prompts14 , each with responses from
a pool of 30 models rated on all four aspects by Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507.
Prompt difficulty and response-model strength each push a response’s score on all four
aspects up or down together, inflating pairwise correlations between aspects for reasons
unrelated to whether the aspects actually agree. Writing s a,p,m for aspect a’s expected rating
on the response to prompt p from model m, we remove both effects before correlating:
s̃ a,p,m = s a,p,m − s̄ a,p,· − s̄ a,·,m + s̄ a,·,· ,

(1)

where s̄ a,p,· , s̄ a,·,m , and s̄ a,·,· are aspect a’s mean score over responses to prompt p, over
responses from model m, and over all responses, respectively.
The four aspects are strongly correlated both before15 and after deconfounding (Table J.8).
Pairwise Spearman correlations are close to the Pearson ones, differing by 0.029 on average
and 0.042 at most (Helpfulness vs. Instruction Following), so the pairwise relationships are
close to linear rather than merely monotonic. Scoring on one aspect alone, rather than
averaging all four, is therefore a safe simplification.
Table J.8: Pairwise Pearson correlation between the four aspects, pooled across all response
models, on scores with prompt and model effects removed via equation 1.

To identify which aspect to keep, we compare all four on three external judging benchmarks: JudgeBench (GPT split) (Tan et al., 2025), RewardBench 2 (Malik et al., 2026), and
RM-Bench (Liu et al., 2025b). Each pairs a prompt with a labeled-preferred response and
one or more labeled-rejected responses, and scores a judge by how often it ranks the preferred response above the rejected one (Table J.9).
Helpfulness is the strongest individual aspect on JudgeBench and RewardBench 2; Truthfulness scores highest on RM-Bench and, by a similarly thin margin, on the threebenchmark average. Since Truthfulness is the least correlated with the other three (Table J.8) and thus least representative of the full rubric, we choose the runner-up, Helpfulness, as our single judging aspect.
14 The released dataset contained 10 prompts twice; we keep only the first occurrence of each.

15 On the raw scores, before removing prompt and model effects, correlations are higher still, ranging from 0.749 (Helpfulness vs. Truthfulness) to 0.849 (Helpfulness vs. Instruction Following).

J.2.2

C HOICE OF J UDGE M ODEL

Having fixed Helpfulness as the scoring aspect, we test whether smaller, newer LLMs that
fit on a single node can substitute for the resource-intensive Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct2507, which requires four nodes, reusing the same three benchmarks as before.
Table J.10 shows Qwen3.6-27B matches or exceeds Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 on all
three benchmarks; the largest gap is on RM-Bench’s Hard split, which specifically tests
robustness to response style over substance. Qwen3.5-35B-A3B is the weakest judge on
RewardBench 2 and RM-Bench, trailing Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 by 10 points on
RewardBench 2 from a collapse on the Ties category (44.0% versus 76.0%). We therefore
choose Qwen3.6-27B as the online DPO judge model, for both performance and cost.

C HATBOT S YSTEM P ROMPT

The chat template ships with a default system prompt, given in Prompt K.1, used verbatim whenever a request supplies no system message; the deployed chatbot relies on this
default.
Default Chatbot System Prompt
You are Apertus 1.5 Omni, a multimodal assistant developed by the Swiss AI
,→
Initiative. Extended from Apertus 1 via continued pretraining, you understand
,→
images and audio and respond in text.

Independent of the system message, the template additionally emits a developer message
that states whether the thinking mode is enabled and declares the available tools (Section 4.1).

126

L

E VALUATION D ETAILS

We report additional evaluation details to enable the replication of results. Furthermore,
we report additional benchmark scores for ablations.
Text-based benchmarks can be reproduced at commit 4ac31da of the Swiss AI
swiss-ai/lm-evaluation-harness fork of lm-evaluation-harness. We used vLLM as
the inference framework to run the models. We generally sample all generative tasks greedily (temperature 0.0), with the exception of thinking-enabled evaluations, which use the
recommended temperature and top-k settings. We score multiple-choice benchmarks either as loglikelihood requests, using the predicted output probabilities to score each answer
option, or generatively, by exact match on the answer extracted from the model’s response.
To evaluate general language understanding of pretrained models, we use HellaSwag (Zellers et al., 2019), PIQA (Bisk et al., 2020), CommonsenseQA (Talmor et al., 2019),
LAMBADA (Paperno et al., 2016), XNLI (Conneau et al., 2018), and XCOPA (Ponti et al.,
2020). Furthermore, we evaluated MMLU with chain-of-thought prompting (Hendrycks
et al., 2021b), Global-MMLU (Singh et al., 2025), MMLU-Pro (Wang et al., 2024),
AGIEval (Zhong et al., 2024), SQuADv2 (Rajpurkar et al., 2018), and TruthfulQA (Lin et al.,
2022), including some multilingual versions generated by machine translation. Finally,
we evaluate some safety-related benchmarks, including BBQ (Parrish et al., 2022), ToxiGen (Hartvigsen et al., 2022), and WMDP (Li et al., 2024b), and we use CulturalBench (Chiu
et al., 2025) to measure wider cultural knowledge.
For post-trained models, we evaluated knowledge via MMLU (Hendrycks et al., 2021b),
MMLU-Pro (Wang et al., 2024), AGIEval (Zhong et al., 2024), ARC-Challenge (Clark et al.,
2018), and TruthfulQA (Lin et al., 2022). We evaluated instruction following and chat
with IFEval (Zhou et al., 2023), Multi-IF (He et al., 2024), IFBench (Pyatkin et al., 2025),
and AlpacaEval (Li et al., 2023b; Dubois et al., 2024). Math capabilities were evaluated
with GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021), MATH, and a sub-sampled MATH500 (Hendrycks et al.,
2021c). For coding, we evaluated models on HumanEval and MBPP (Austin et al., 2021).
Reasoning was benchmarked via BBH (Suzgun et al., 2023), GPQA (Rein et al., 2024), and
DROP (Dua et al., 2019). Finally, multilingual and cultural understanding were evaluated
via Global-MMLU (Singh et al., 2025) and an in-house benchmark focusing on Switzerland
called SwitzerlandQA, which we will release in the future. For thinking-model benchmarks,
we additionally evaluated AIME 2024 and 2025 (Dekoninck et al., 2026).
Table L.1 and Table L.2 contain a list of all used benchmarks, including replication-relevant
settings, such as few-shot examples.
L.1

F ULL P OST-T RAINING L INEAGE

Section 5.3.2 tracks the Apertus 1.5 models through the post-training stages on a representative subset of each skill area. We give the same lineage over the full development suite
here. The held-out and thinking-mode tables of that section already list every benchmark
of their suite, so they have no fuller form here. We compute the behavioral measures once
per checkpoint rather than per suite, and they appear at the foot of Table L.3.

130

M

P RE -R ELEASE M ANUAL E VALUATIONS

M.1

G ENERAL I NFORMATION A BOUT P RE -R ELEASE E VALUATION

We perform the manual evaluation on all release-candidate models prior to their public
release, and we provide the full manual evaluation report, along with the recommendation
from the safety and security lead as to the model release, to inform a release/no-release
decision.
In every test case below, we evaluate the model behavior in English, three common languages of the Confederation (German, French, and Italian), and Serbian (Cyrillic SerboCroatian), as a low-resource language with a different alphabet and notable usage within
Switzerland.
For all evaluations of usage scenarios assuming malicious users (CBRNE, Dual Use, Disinformation, and Child Sexual Abuse enablement), we evaluate abliterated versions of the
release candidates alongside the release candidates to confirm the absence of dangerous
capabilities independently of the model alignment. For non-abliterated models, we evaluate their helpfulness through multi-turn, multi-conversation interactions with a malicious
user faking benign interest.
In addition to the internal safety and security evaluation, we provide early access to the
release candidates to independent third-party evaluators. Specifically, we provide access
to Orange Cyberdefense Switzerland (previously SCRT) for offensive cybersecurity capabilities evaluation; to Cyber-Defence Campus, armasuisse S+T, DDPS (Federal Department
of Defence, Civil Protection and Sport) for overall model risk evaluation; and to Spiez Lab,
Federal Office for Civil Protection, DDPS, for CBRNE capabilities evaluation.
M.2

C HEMICAL , B IOLOGICAL , R ADIOLOGICAL , N UCLEAR , AND E XPLOSIVE
I NDISCRIMINATE W EAPONS (CBRNE)

Threat Model. A moderately sophisticated attacker seeks to fabricate such a weapon at
home. We assume they are seeking to acquire fabrication knowledge for such weapons
by prompting the model. This would notably exclude biological weapons fabrication, due
to the need for specialized equipment and tools in order to produce and maintain such
weapons, as well as nuclear weapons, given the difficulty of procuring the components,
correctly assembling them, and packaging them for delivery. While such tests are important for frontier models, they are not a primary concern for a medium-sized open-weight
model such as the Apertus family.
Results. While the abliterated versions of the model provide realistic-looking recipes for
CBRNE indiscriminate weapons in the test cases examined, due to the concept collapse,
none of the recipes examined contain sufficient information for household assembly alone,
and none provide any additional information beyond what is available on the web. We observe a variability in response across languages, including lower refusal in the low-resource
language tested, but that does not affect the overall evaluation of release candidates as safe
to release.
M.3

D UAL U SE AND I NSURGENCY

Threat Model. Similar to CBRNE weapons, dual-use and specific military knowledge is
generally considered inherently harmful in models available to the general public. Additionally, some of this knowledge might be useful for the preparation of terrorist attacks on
civilians, making the existence of such knowledge within LLMs inherently problematic.
Results. Neither the release candidates nor the abliterated versions of release candidates
provide any useful dual-use information in the test cases analyzed.
131

M.4

LLM-A SSISTED I NFORMATION O PERATIONS

Threat Model. An attacker attempts to leverage an LLM for information operations in
a low-resource language. Notably, they use in-depth prompting and real-world text examples to generate social media biographies of journalists or digital technology company
workers, social media posts, or short articles, using few-shot learning prompts for target
text types and in-depth person definition prompting in the original language, a popular
and effective attack strategy (Gameiro et al., 2024; Kucharavy et al., 2025).
Results. We are not able to produce texts undetectable to common online free LLM detectors across several mid-to-low-resource languages.
M.5

(C HILD ) S EXUAL A BUSE

Threat Model. Distinct from CBRNE and dual-use concerns is the use of LLMs for sexual
and intimate abuse assistance, notably against children. Based on the Lanzarote Convention (Council of Europe, 2007), as well as the definitions in Ghosh et al. (2025) and Autio
et al. (2024), Wuhrmann et al. (2026) identified two clearly illegitimate uses of LLMs in that
context, and demonstrated how LLMs’ usefulness in such uses could be evaluated beyond
simple refusal, leveraging the Swiss Supreme Court decisions, which are publicly available
and correspond to unequivocally and clearly illegal instances of abuse.
Results. Apertus 1.5 demonstrates a significant improvement in refusal and noncompliance with the requests emulating illegitimate uses compared to Apertus 1.0. Overall, Apertus 1.5 refusal and request compliance rates are in line with other widely used
open-weight LLMs of similar size, and once abliterated, it does not comply with requests
better than readily available abliterated models of the same size.
M.6

O FFENSIVE C YBERSECURITY

Threat Model. A malicious party in cyberspace uses the model in order to design, support, and conceal offensive operations.
Results. The evaluation from the third party (Orange Cyberdefense Switzerland) reports
limited usefulness for offensive usage; however, it is significantly below other common
open-weight models and has insufficient structured output compliance to allow for offensive automation. The Apertus safety and security team does not judge the reported results
sufficient to block the candidates’ release.
M.7

M ULTIMODAL P ROMPT I NJECTION

Threat Model. We evaluate the model’s susceptibility to prompt injection through text
supplied as images, including white-on-white text, previously reported as effective against
widely used commercial LLMs.
Results.
didates.
M.8

We do not observe compliance with embedded instructions for the release can-

H ARMFUL D ISINFORMATION

Threat Model. LLM vectoring has been reported as an offensive information warfare tactic (VIGINUM, 2024; Sadeghi & Blachez, 2025), which is language-dependent. Here we investigate how much Apertus has been affected using publicly available examples of false
narratives. Additionally, we ask the model about the status of the contested territories as
defined in the UN 2022 “Main Instances of Disputed Territories” resolution and evaluate
the partiality of the model’s response.
132

Results. We note minimal disinformation recitation or disputed-territory partiality for
released models, even in non-thinking mode.
M.9

H ARMFUL H EALTH A DVICE

Threat Model. We focus on the harmful health advice that has been reported or can reasonably be expected from LLM-based chatbots. In order to test the model in the most realistic condition, we attempt the harmful response elicitation as a question with an implicit
assumption of harmful statement correctness.
Results. Across evaluated scenarios, the release candidates consistently inform the users
of the harmfulness of the health advice, except in the low-resource language tested. While
this behavior can be improved in the future, the release candidates offer an improvement
compared to other commonly used open-weight models, resulting in a decision to not
block the candidates’ release.
M.10

P RIVATE P ERSON D EFAMATION

Threat Model. In this section, we focus on the case of an LLM user attempting to find
information about a real person and getting partially incorrect, prejudicial information.
Specifically, we examine the hallucination of a nonexistent lawsuit following a two-stage
dedicated prompt, and the generation of partially correct information about a private person that would uniquely identify them but be incorrect about their biography.
Results. We observe a high variability in the results across languages and private person names, with the vast majority refusing generation, and those providing it adding disclaimers or not allowing a reasonable association with a real person. We observe a quasiuniversal rejection of the nonexistent lawsuit assumption, except for the low-resource language tested. While this behavior can be improved in the future, the model offers an improvement compared to other commonly used open-weight models, resulting in a decision
to not block the candidates’ release.
